Gradualism to persist elsewhere as Fed policy rates step down

Core inflation looks to be firming outside US this quarter

• China exports sag without a domestic demand offset

• Next week: Soft July China data; tame US core CPI; RBNZ eases

Recent discussion in these pages has focused on the shifting risk bias around our global narrative of sustained growth resilience, sticky inflation, and shallow central bank rate normalization. 1H24 performance hewed closely to these views: global GDP and core CPI inflation both exceeded expectations as DM central banks generally remained on hold. But momentum dipped around midyear, removing an earlier upside risk bias to our forecasts. This cycle has been replete with short-term momentum swings and there are good reasons to place a high noise-to-signal ratio on this year’s front-loaded growth and inflation profile. Through this noise, however, we see reasons to be concerned that weakening global manufacturing and US labor demand may be signaling a shift toward private sector caution.

These developments have not altered our baseline views on any major economy recently—with the notable exceptions of growth revisions to China (down) and Japan (up). In our updated scenario tree, we reflect recent news, modestly raising the assessed risk of a 2H24 US/global recession to 35% (Figure 1). A more substantial reassessment of the interest rate outlook has been concentrated in the US. We now expect the Fed to speed up rate normalization, delivering roughly 100bp of easing by year-end. Changes elsewhere are thus far limited to EM. Reflecting this shift, the cumulative probability we assign to our two high-for-long rate scenarios (too darn hot and high for real long) has been lowered to 30%, down from 50% as recently as two months ago.

Three underlying elements are worth highlighting to explain our focus on US interest rate risk.

Correlated risk should shake gradualism. Across the globe, central banks have guided toward a more cautious rate normalization than growth-inflation dynamics would warrant. We see this gradualist guidance through the lens of a risk management strategy in which optimism about growth resilience has remained high despite perceived restrictive stances and disinflation confidence has been tempered by tight labor markets and elevated wage gains. Against this backdrop, moderate but correlated shifts downward in growth and inflation risk can have a material impact on central bank behavior.

US at a different cyclical juncture. As we move through 2024, US (and China) policymakers are managing a demand downshift while most other major economies are tracking an anticipated lift from last year’s cyclical weakness (Figure 2). Against that backdrop, the Fed is likely to be particularly sensitive to any indication that the normalization in US labor demand amplifies downward dynamics in the broader economy.

US supply side delivers dividends. Global core inflation is still tracking our forecast for an elevated 3% 2024 rise as we look through the noisy 1Q24 upside and 2Q24 downside surprise. However, there has been a material positive shift in the risk profile on the US inflation backdrop as strong supply side performance combines with moderating labor demand to produce a significant easing in labor market conditions. US wage inflation is now slowing in a manner not seen in other DM economies. Combined with sustained solid productivity gains, US unit labor costs have realigned to a level broadly consistent with the Fed’s inflation target.

We believe these developments warrant a pickup in the cadence of Fed easing, but our forecast for a 50bp cut in September will likely require reinforcement of this risk bias shift in upcoming releases. Central in this discussion is that a more front-loaded Fed easing that responds to recession risks not realized will improve the 2025 growth outlook. While recognizing several uncertainties related to geopolitics and the US election, we continue to forecast both US and global GDP to rise at a modestly above-potential pace next year.

## July core inflation firms outside the US

After a volatile first two quarters in which global core CPI (ex China and Türkiye) rose 3.4%ar, we look for a limited moderation to 2.8%ar in 2H24. Our expectation for continued sticky inflation rests on the view that an incomplete decline in global service price inflation is tempered by modest firming in core goods inflation. At the same time, a regional rotation is anticipated as an unwind of an early year spike and more

easing in labor cost pressures is expected to produce a relatively large slide in 2H24 US core inflation. Signs of a cooling in US services price inflation was evident in June and we expect it to be reinforced in next week’s July release. July reports from nearly half of our GDW universe are already available and show core CPI gains tracking a 0.3%m/m rise. Last week’s Euro area print showed core HICP rose at a 3.7%ar in the three months to July, and core inflation firmed across much of EM although the source of upside varies across regions (Figure 3).

## European gradualism remains in place

The boost to US supply contrasts with weak performance in Europe, where labor markets are tight and wage inflation remains elevated. There is therefore no reason to shift views about inflation risk and central bank behavior. Experience shows that the pass-through of Fed policy changes is limited in the absence of a synchronized shift in growth and/or financial market conditions.

We thus stick to our forecast for a quarterly pace of 25bp cuts for the ECB and BoE, expecting them to ease next in September and November, respectively. The main risk to an earlier move comes from domestic downside growth surprises—with risks related to the Euro area PMI drop and UK riots worth monitoring. Norges Bank, meanwhile, is widely expected to be on hold next week despite a substantial downside surprise in this week’s July core inflation reading. Currency weakness is a focal point for the Committee, and the recent sell-off biases near-term risk in the direction of a hike. We still expect the next move to be a December ease but recognize Norges Bank as a Western Europe hawkish outlier.

## More than an EM ripple, less than a tide

By most benchmarks, EM monetary policy stances are sufficiently restrictive to warrant easing if our macroeconomic forecasts materialize. This is true even in economies that have eased markedly to date. Still, there is hesitancy due to multiple domestic risks not reflected in DM gradualism guidance: political and policy risks (e.g., Brazil, Mexico, and South

Bruce Kasman (1-212) 834-5515 bruce.c.kasman@jpmorgan.com JPMorgan Chase Bank NA Joseph Lupton (1-212) 834-5735 joseph.p.lupton@jpmorgan.com

Africa); fears of triggering FX and financial instability where the buffer against US rates is low (most of EM Asia); and structural rather than cyclical factors holding growth down (CEE). Faced with these constraints, a more front-loaded Fed easing opens only modest space for earlier EM cuts. We have added 50bp of cuts in India and Indonesia and a projected 50bp easing in South Africa has been brought forward. Easing in Brazil and Mexico that was removed amid FX depreciation pressures has been restored.

Financial market volatility is an additional near-term constraint on EM easing. The unwind of carry trades has hit EM asset prices, portfolio flows, and exchange rates. While credit and inter-bank markets have remained calm, several central banks have expended significant reserves in FX intervention. On Thursday, Mexico’s Banxico delivered the expected 25bp rate cut, setting aside concerns over exchange rate pressures with the rise in downside risks to near-term growth. The peso, which had been appreciating this week in response to greater assurance of a tight 2025 budget and amendments to the proposed legislative changes, continued to strengthen after the cut. This could be a signal that EM financial pressures are abating. However, we think it’s unlikely that any material action will be forthcoming from EM central bankers before the September FOMC decision.

## Inflation, politics, key to 2025 EM easing

While policy rates are moving lower in EM, the path of real rates is expected to remain elevated vis à vis our assessment of macroeconomic fundamentals. The caution embedded in our forecast partly reflects inflation uncertainty, which remains a dominant concern across Latam and CEE. Measures of slack suggest inflation should move lower, but concern about poor supply-side performance and sticky inflation outcomes does not provide confidence on the path ahead. With this uncertainty elevated, central banks (and our forecasts) put less weight on indicators of slack and more on inflation, including as a lens for assessing capacity.

In addition, EM growth and financial conditions can be severely affected by changes in US trade, immigration, and foreign policies. The US election risk is likely to weigh heavily in the considerations of EM central banks for some time to come. Against this backdrop, it is not a surprise that the forecasted EM rate differential to the Fed has widened, awaiting clarity on these two key sources of risk (Figure 4).

## Good news for Japan’s consumers

Incoming data bolster our confidence that Japan’s economy is set to lift, and we see GDP rising at a roughly 2%ar through the middle quarters of this year. Absent a realization of downside growth risk elsewhere, the path ahead looks good for sustained above-potential growth. A key driver of this lift is the restoration of household purchasing power following a long period in which wage gains lagged inflation. The June wage data showed the largest rise since the mid-1990s, supported by strong summer bonuses and a trend increase in scheduled earnings. Real wage gains turned positive for the first time in over two years. With these gains, combined with temporary tax cuts, households should see a material boost to labor incomes. Further good news came from the BoJ CAI, which confirmed a 2Q consumption rebound after the 1Q slump, while SME sentiment also moved higher. Reflecting these improvements, we look for a 2%ar gain in next week’s 2Q GDP report. The unfolding virtuous cycle should keep the BoJ on a tightening path that takes the policy rate to 1% in 2025. However, it is likely to remain patient—we look for the next hike near year-end—as it awaits a period of financial market stability.

## Downside risks to China growth

The first batch of July data raise downside risks for a Chinese economy already expected to grow at a modest 4.5%ar in 2H24. Exports fell sharply in July. The breadth of the decline across trading partners raises larger questions on global growth but is a clear concern for an economy currently dependent on external sources of demand. Next week’s July data dump should reinforce this point as an expected softening in factory output gains is expected to be accompanied by sub-par retail and FAI growth. The government is stepping up its policy response, but the adjustment remains gradual. The State Council this week announced 20 measures to boost consumption and an earlier ban on online tutoring seemed to be relaxed. Monetary policy is also easing, but the overall policy response looks insufficient to offset downside risks to nearterm growth.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our estimate for 3Q24 global real GDP growth moved sideways this week at 2.1%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster similarly held at 2.0%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth.

Nearly half of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 3Q growth in about 60% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests global capex growth will stall this quarter after growing 2.8%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

The manufacturing and services PMI gap widened notably last month, but the all-industry index remains consistent with a 3%ar gain in GDP. Other July surveys look encouraging: global business confidence rebounded, and consumer confidence edged up after a recent slide. G-4 credit conditions also look to be nearing stabilization.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds sold off modestly this week amid some fading of immediate recession fears and as risk assets found some support. In the US, the decline in initial claims assuaged some fears over the labor market weakness for the time being. We continue to see a 50bp cut at the September meeting, but while market pricing is roughly halfway between a 25bp and 50bp cut, the August labor market report will be key. We remain patient on outright duration, and keep 5s/30s steepeners as a lower-beta long with better carry characteristics ahead of the first cut.

In the Euro area, activity data continue to portray a picture of subdued growth. While the repricing of Fed expectations has raised questions over a faster pace of ECB cuts, the relatively sparse commentary by Board members suggests cuts still depend on the disinflation process remaining on track. We stay neutral duration and look for opportunities to re-enter longs, and keep 2Yx2Y/15Yx15Y EUR swap curve steepeners as a bullish proxy. In the UK, the riots that broke out over the past weeks pose some near-term downside risk to growth, but the 2011 experience did not leave a significant data imprint. We keep fronts/reds SONIA curve flatteners and stay received 5Yx5Y SONIA vs. SOFR. In Japan, with issuance light and investor conviction that front-end rates are too low, we close 10s/30s JGB curve steepeners.

In EM, the extreme volatility hit EM amid concerns over recession fears and unwinds of yen-denominated carry trades. In local bonds, we have increased our duration stance on select ‘safer’ markets such as Czechia. Overall we stay neutral EM local bonds, with OWs in India, Czechia, Poland, Türkiye and Uruguay offset by an UW in Chile as well as a short duration overlay (EM Fixed Income Focus, Aug 6<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

US HG spreads widened on weaker economic and earnings data. The daily spread change volatility for the HG index rose to 4.3bp, a level last seen during the height of regional banking crisis in March 2023. For HG bond spreads the move lower in UST yields is a negative near term as yield buyers step back and everyone re-assesses valuations. However, investors are not shying away from the asset class as evidenced by the busiest day of the year for new issue activity this past week which showed a willingness to commit fresh capital at wider spreads. Part of what may be underlying this demand is that a) HG Corps spreads don’t look as rich anymore versus MBS and b) reinvestment needs are steadily climbing as both coupon and maturity payments accelerate. We remain comfortable with our 110bp year-end spread target

(CMOS, Aug 9<sup>th</sup>).

Euro credit markets round tripped this week, with euro IG and euro HY spreads ending essentially flat since last Friday having been as much as 8bp and 22bp wider on Monday. However, in our view, the consolidation in the last few days may only be a temporary respite. When we first turned cautious two weeks ago, it was not based on an expected unwind of the yen carry trade, but on weak guidance around consumer facing sectors during earnings season combined with a fading of the economic momentum from the first half of the year. We continue to believe that euro credit needs to price more recession risk. As a result, we think that the recent weakness marks the transition into a higher volatility and wider spread regime over the end of the year, rather than a short-lived dip buying opportunity (European Credit Weekly, Aug 9<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns
![](images/10a677ccd2a8357c1b101ede37607db11a7cc78c3061c5d906c19c7f58f76242.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

No rest from restructuring. The 12m trailing European HY default rate dropped by 17bp to 2.62% in July, despite two companies defaulting on a combined €2.225bn. A busy few months for restructuring activity pushed the 6m annualised rate up to 4%, from a recent low of 1.3% in March. At 8.5%, the distressed ratio is in this middle of this year’s range, even with €8.6bn of restructured bonds leaving the index over the period. Average recoveries jumped 4%-pts to 47% in July, due to the near-zero recoveries on COFP bonds dropping out of the data (High Yield Talking Points, Aug 6<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

Weeks where decades happen. July/Aug 2024 delivered a vol shock for FX, culminating in a huge JPY rally and attendant drawdown on the favored FX carry trade. We revise our USD/JPY forecasts to 144 for 2Q’25, which implies JPY consolidation from here. Carry trades have erased YTD gains; we estimate 65-75% of positioning being unwound. Looking forward, FX carry’s prospects remain challenged. USD’s response has been mixed. But USD losses are ultimately fairly shallow, in line with what we wrote last month. And we still see reason for optimism on USD’s medium-term prospects. RoW growth is not conducive to sustained USD selling.

Trade recommendations: Sell NZD/NOK. Rotate short-NZD basket (vs USD, JPY) to NZD/USD outright. G10 forecasts: USD/JPY 4Q 146 (156), 2Q’25 144 (154). EUR/USD 4Q 1.09, 2Q’25 1.12. GBP/USD 4Q 1.30 (1.31), 2Q’25 1.35. AUD/USD 4Q 0.67 (0.68), 2Q’25 0.68. USD/CAD 4Q 1.35 (1.36), 2Q’25 1.33 (1.34). EM: USD/CNY 4Q 7.15 (7.30), 2Q'25 7.05 (7.20). USD/INR 4Q 83.90 (83.80), 2Q'25 82.50 (84.00). USD/MXN 4Q 17.75, 2Q'25 17.50. USD/BRL 4Q 5.20, 2Q'25 5.10 (KCV, Aug 9<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

Global commodity open interest value slides to a fivemonth low amid macro contagion. Outflows were mainly concentrated across crude oil markets and precious metals while petroleum products and grains & oilseeds market registered modest inflows over the week. Net investor length across agri markets declined to the lowest level since January, while energy markets positions almost halved. Base and precious metals net length decreased, with inflows in aluminum largely offset by outflows in lead. Overall, positioning has cleaned up across base metals after sharp investor de-risking in the last month. We see a significant downside risk to the total sugarcane crush this season. In this edition we have incorporated estimates of net investor positioning using JPM’s Global QDS Research high frequency CFTC investor net length forecasts (Commodity Market Positioning & Flows, Aug 5<sup>th</sup>).

Oversold levels present consumer coverage opportunities across the agri complex. Positioning across US traded agri markets is at the weakest seasonal level on record, reflective of expectations for rising US grain and oilseed inventories on the month via record-high yields – albeit underpricing inventory draws across other major exporters. The BCOM Agri index is now trading at a 3Y low. Off these price lows, and in the absence of a recessionary outlook, we see fundamental discounts across grain, sugar and palm oil markets in particular and consider that agri markets offer value for consumers to extend forward price coverage. We stay long the agri complex via an index. Our price forecasts sit above futures curves, and markets are discounting supply-side and geopolitical risks which remain heightened in our view. Our highestconviction long underlyings are Sugar, Wheat and Corn (Agricultural Markets Weekly, Aug 9<sup>th</sup>).

## Gradualism under pressure

• Central bank gradualism under pressure as balance of risks shifts

• Activity data remain solid but there are hints of a private sector turn to caution

• We assess probability of 2H24 US/global recession up modestly to 35%

Increase in US recession probability comes with easing in labor market pressures not evident elsewhere

• The interaction of these risk shifts warrants a Fed break from gradualism

Markets have recently priced a considerably faster pace of central bank easing, partly reflecting rising concern of a US/ global recession, realization of which would almost certainly produce a sharp and immediate easing. As we noted last week, important elements of our growth forecast are being challenged. The latest business surveys suggest a loss of momentum in global manufacturing and in the Euro area, weak links in the expansion that we have expected to lift this year. US news also hints at a sharper-than-expected weakening in labor demand and early signs of labor shedding. These forces warrant increased concern but are tempered by continued solid gains in overall activity led by the service sector. More fundamentally, the vulnerabilities normally associated with a recession break—sustained profit margin compression or credit market stress, and energy or financial market shocks—are notably absent. As such, we have only modestly increased our assessment of near-term recession risk, raising the probability of a US/global recession starting before yearend to 35%, up from 25% in our midyear outlook (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Global outlook scenarios, 2024-25
![](images/bae564761326018c329fb0222552a84d298cc14e90f01e304459ce77cc5fcfb1.jpg)

This modest increase in our assessment of recession risk contrasts with a more substantial reassessment we are making to the interest rate outlook. Indeed, the cumulative probability we assign to two high-for-long rate scenarios (too darn hot and high for real long) in our scenario tree have been lowered to 30%, down from a 50% assessment as recently as two months ago. The driver of this more substantial shift is the correlated shifts in growth and inflation risk that are shaking the gradualism narrative in current central bank rate guidance. Specifically, there has been a material positive shift in the risk profile on US inflation as strong supply side performance combines with moderating labor demand to ease labor market pressure. US wage inflation is now slowing in a manner not seen in other DM economies. Combined with sustained solid productivity gains, US unit labor costs have realigned to a level broadly consistent with the Fed’s inflation target (Figure 2).

Figure 2: US unit labor costs and core PCE % change, saar
![](images/a27b860444d2230775f19b98c704a1e79dbfa899887437ed3af6c6c7b8164309.jpg)

While much of the debate around the Fed response to softer labor demand relates to rising growth risk, it is the interaction of shifting growth and inflation risk that should shake the Fed’s gradualist guidance. Easing labor market conditions increase confidence both that service price inflation will move lower and that the Fed’s current policy stance is restrictive. Importantly, it reduces lingering concerns that inflation salience and elevated wage inflation could generate a feedback loop that entrenches inflation and undermines Fed credibility. Taken together these developments warrant a break from gradualism and we expect the Fed to make a level adjustment in its policy stance that lowers policy rates by at least 100bp through year-end.

Two additional points are worth noting. First and foremost, an early easing cycle that responds to shifting risks on growth and inflation—but not the realization of a recession—likely improves the outlook for growth looking to next year. While recognizing additional uncertainties related to the political backdrop, we have not altered our assessment of the probabil ity of a recession by the end of next year, which remains at 45%. Second, a supply-side easing in labor market conditions and moderating wage inflation is not evident elsewhere. Experience shows that the pass-through of Fed policy changes to other economies is limited in the absence of a synchronized shift in macroeconomic fundamentals and/or financial market conditions (Figure 3). As a result, there is a good chance that the shift away from gradualism we now expect from the Fed will not be reflected more broadly.

Figure 3: Policy rates
![](images/53a1df9920302377fc15b8bcb229b4e9e07cad7a1aac29cbcf6b894b11f13c3d.jpg)

## Gradualism linked to CB risk assessment

After tightening nearly 500bp through the middle of last year, DM central banks outside Japan have turned cautious. While DM core inflation rates have fallen sharply from their peak— dropping to 3.0% in the 12-months through June—average DM policy rates outside Japan are only 10bp off their highs. This caution partly reflects the early year rise in DM core inflation performance which surprised central bankers. However, a cautious move to unwind tightening reflects more than realized macro performance. This point is brought home in a recent study in which we showed that variations in estimated output and inflation gaps explain much of the cross-country variation in policy rates across the globe. The study also shows that policy rates are almost uniformly higher than macroeconomic fundamentals warrant, as described by a balanced Taylor rule (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Policy rate 2Q24, Taylor vs actual
![](images/796bf5c705bf685bd2449abaca4080465d5edca2d6bed0bad2233543daff610e.jpg)

We interpret the caution displayed by central banks through the lens of their management of risk which promotes gradualism. Their assessment along three dimensions underscores the general guidance towards gradualism.

A lack of confidence on last mile. Most large central banks anticipate inflation to return close to 2% by the end of next year. However, this optimism has been tempered, thus far, by the sticky service price inflation. Concerns that service price inflation remains high is linked to still tight labor markets and elevated wage inflation which is currently not aligned with delivering the last mile of disinflation (Figure 5).

Figure 5: DM core CPI measures and wage growth %oya in specified CPI measure; triangles denote latest 4qtrs
![](images/1322dfd8c3d894308b76baa286370f3dc0a47715e4feafe8f4c816e89afbbfe8.jpg)
Source: National sources, J.P. Morgan. Details on request. Sample: 2001-2019.

Restrictive stances not seen as a threat to growth. Central bankers describe their stance as restrictive, emphasizing that policy rates are elevated relative to estimates of the neutral rate. At the same time, however, they do not view their restrictive stances as a threat to the expansion against the backdrop of broadly easy financial conditions. That the inflation decline forecasted by major central banks over the coming year is generally not associated with policy delivering a period of sub-par growth underscores their willingness to move gradually towards rate normalization.

Credibility is a precious asset. Chair Powell has been vocal in emphasizing the two-sided risk profile around the speed of policy normalization. The consequences of easing too late and generating an unnecessary recession are well appreciated. But it is important to recognize the value central banks place on maintaining their credibility through anchoring inflation expectations close to target. While medium-term expectations generally remain well behaved, short-term expectations have drifted higher (Figure 6). The risk that an early easing could promote an interaction between elevated wage inflation and expectations that embeds an inflation overshoot is a concern shared broadly by central bankers.

Figure 6: 1y1y inflation swaps
![](images/7c4407c9823bbb0a3a9165ed47538ccc4e29ed16acd24b6f336b64e828f23cf0.jpg)

## Data still track solid growth, sticky inflation

With judgments about risk providing a key foundation for gradualist guidance, it should be no surprise that a significant shift in growth and inflation risk can alter the policy normalization path. An interesting feature of the current environment is that neither activity nor inflation readings are sending a clear-cut signal of a material weakness ahead at midyear.

We anticipated that the global expansion would move through an orderly transition this year as rotations in demand towards goods-producing industries and away from the US take hold amidst a moderation to a trend-like pace of global growth. Performance during 1H24 has generally aligned with this view. Although global industry started off the year on a weak note, production gains accelerated to a 2.4%ar last quarter, supported in part by a pickup in both retail spending and capex (Figure 7). Looking past volatile 1H24 performance in China, global GDP has increased at a modestly above-potential pace both quarters. The US has moderated to a still solid 2.1%ar 1H24 pace. A return to roughly 1% growth in Western Europe and strong gains recorded across most of EM outside China have offset this moderation on a global basis.

Figure 7: Global consumer spending
%3m, saar, both scales; dashed is global ex. China
![](images/2a4c12d58b8c56bb4b78b0c654985805646812f7dbf865edd378b5885d7c4c8a.jpg)

It is too early to gauge current quarter performance but the activity readings at midyear are consistent with sustained solid growth momentum. This is the message from the July all-industry global PMI which continues to track GDP growth at close to 3% (Figure 8). Our GDP nowcaster aligns with our forecast for a 2%ar gain this quarter. In the US, our nowcaster tracks current quarter growth close to 2% while the Atlanta Fed tracker stands close to 3%.

Figure 8: Global all-industry PMI and GDP
![](images/031dc017c807f980b30f3af8d0a22c6b5db2dd5a312c602074ec3d25f6c3e105.jpg)

Through a sharp up-and-down pattern through the first two quarters of the year, global core CPI rose at a 3.2%ar, slightly above our forecast at the start of the year. In a recent report we argued that there will be limited moderation during 2H24. Global goods price pressures appear to be building, at the same time, global service price inflation is moderating at midyear (Figure 9). Importantly, the underlying deceleration in service price inflation is expected to be limited in an environment in which labor costs continue to rise at an elevated pace and global service sector growth remains firm (Figure 10). As such, we expect global core inflation to remain close to a 3%ar in 2H24, a substantial but incomplete slide from its 2021-23 heights.

Figure 9: Global core CPI, ex China and Türkiye
![](images/15099b07b73f2c3391ce3af18486bf8255e311cfe811ac0bb66643099c64b0b2.jpg)

Figure 10: DM unemployment rate and wages
![](images/448de6b2cc90e1e708cb2209ace6795f086f907bbd5b98701553ecc71702e2dd.jpg)

## The concern relates to signals on behavior

Incoming activity readings still align with our views on growth, and we have not made material forecast changes to our GDP forecasts in recent weeks. That said, there are concerns being raised regarding private sector behavior in highfrequency indicators, which does increase our perception of downside risk.

The midyear slide in global manufacturing surveys and signs of a loss in Euro area momentum at midyear challenge our view on resilience and a broadening global growth base. The details of the global PMI point to a sharp drop in new orders (Figure 11). Combined with a recent slide in our manufacturing expectations index, the report contrasts with our view that a recovery in global capex growth is now underway. Concentrated weakness in Germany within the Euro area is not a surprise, but raises questions around an expected pickup in regional consumption around midyear is taking hold (Figure 12).

Figure 11: Global goods demand indicators
![](images/9a6d03b9a374282a162c1369be0a46d94d38765dd532dd98d51d2b58e637f7d6.jpg)

Figure 12: Euro area retail sales and consumer spending %q/q, saar; marker is J.P. Morgan forecast for 2Q consumer spending
![](images/1f57915b4b7a7335af9e057c239ba7023fb459c5a524677a8875f4deff33a844.jpg)

A sharp slowing in US employment gains last month was likely magnified by a weather-related drag, but there is growing evidence of a significant softening in labor demand. Until recently, we have attributed the moderation in job growth and rise in unemployment to a mix of normalizing labor demand amidst sustained strong gains in supply. The latest labor market data continue to point to strong supply, but also point to a notable increase in the

flow of workers from employed to unemployed—a development which hints at a more troubling behavioral shift toward job shedding (Figure 13).

Figure 13: US flows into unemployment
![](images/f7e31afdee453d1918fb34a24735fc8345fc4c5ef4e539d8f2383b31e58ed83f.jpg)

## The risk shift on inflation is US centric

While inflation news globally is not sending a decisive signal, an important shift in relative performance appears to be taking hold. The US stands alone among major economies in delivering a recovery in which GDP has risen above its prepandemic potential path. However, the US has also delivered the strongest supply-side performance with both labor supply and productivity gains now rising above its pre-pandemic pace (Figure 14). As we turned into the new year, it was uncertain what impact the combined strength in US demand and supply would have on labor markets. With the US unemployment rate up 0.6%-pt this year, positive supply side dynamics are dominating, generating an easing in labor market pressures.

Figure 14: DM labor productivity
![](images/ede9bbd077c152045cff201ea04a32b939e93f0bcc40c0cb67ee91dcdf5f5041.jpg)

In addition to the rise in the US unemployment rate the latest readings show that US labor cost pressures are easing—both in absolute terms and relative to other DM economies. Despite sustained strong employment gains, a clearly established downtrend in wage inflation is now in place not evident elsewhere (Figure 15). With the US also generating a solid 2%ar gain in productivity over the past two years, unit labor costs have been pushed down to a level broadly consistent with the Fed’s inflation objective (Figure 2).

Figure 15: DM wages %oya; Euro area and Sweden thru 1Q, rest thru 2Q
![](images/2f1d6be4ff2a7a6fa43d4a569c884460623a96689aae956a1d8c4dcd4ef08a64.jpg)

## Correlated risk shift shakes Fed gradualism

To a large degree discussion around the recent market repricing of DM central bank easing relates to growth risks from softening US labor markets and slipping Euro area business surveys. In this regard, it is important to recognize that even a modest increase in recession risk will have a material impact on the “mean” pricing of the path ahead as a recession would almost certainly precipitate dramatic easing. However, the repricing of the US Fed has been most significant—as has the change in our rate forecast for the coming year—because of the correlated shifts in risk on growth and inflation.

The US stands alone among DM economies in the recent easing in labor market tightness and in its deceleration in wage inflation. This shift should have a significant direct impact on raising the Fed’s confidence that service price inflation will move lower over the coming quarters. Moderating wage inflation should also reduce concerns that inflation salience may generate an inflation firming feedback loop through expectations. While each of these channels may be shifting modestly, taken together they are likely to prompt a substantial shift away from the current Fed guidance of gradualism. As such, we now expect the Fed to make a level adjustment in its policy stance starting in September that lowers policy rates by at least 100bp through year-end.

## And just like that: Fed to make room for more EM cuts

We have changed our Fed rate call markedly: more front-loaded cuts to take FFR to 3% by Jul 2025

• In response, we have front-loaded cuts in many EM economies and deepened cuts in some

EM Asia should gain the most space, but Brazil, Mexico, and South Africa should also benefit

• Still, CB stances will remain restrictive relative to growth-inflation dynamics even by end-2025

Hesitance toward more aggressive easing reflects multiple risks, including those surrounding US elections

If such risks do not materialize, more EM easing is likely next year

For some time we have been arguing that there are two paths to the forthcoming Fed easing. In the first of these, disinflation is accompanied by a strong labor market, eliciting calibrated and drawn out easing. In the second, the labor market would also break, prompting deeper and more front-loaded easing. Last Friday, the US July jobs report delivered what could be the start of such a break: non-farm payrolls (NFP) were up just 114k—significantly less than the past six months’ run rate of 218k—and the unemployment rate ticked up for a sixth consecutive month to 4.3%, almost a full %-pt above its cycle low. The downshift in NFP growth may well reverse, at least partly, as weather-related cutbacks fade. But broader labor market dynamics such as the softening in wage growth—which could be heralding further (services) disinflation—and the rise in layoffs were also worrisome.

Figure 1: Policy rate forecast revision: US %p.a.; dotted line shows revised JPM forecast
![](images/87dfdf98ed167acf85775e733db9665c7baf639768b415cb2c3c4390cd73405e.jpg)
With a rise in downside risks on both employment and inflation, our US economists have pulled forward and added to projected Fed easing. We now look a 50bp cut at both the September and November meetings, and a 25bp cut at every meeting thereafter to reach a terminal rate of 3% by July 2025 (Figure 1). Moreover, the US yield curve has also shifted down in anticipation of deeper Fed cuts, and our strategists

now expect 10-yr US yields to fall to 3.15% and USDEUR to depreciate to 1.12 by mid-2025.

## EM: One size does not fit all

How do these moves affect EM central banks? The changes have come in the midst of a simultaneous unwinding of the yen carry trade, rise in geopolitical tensions, and rotation out of IT-related stocks. This has adversely affected global equity prices, EM flows, and exchange rates. Several central banks have already expended significant reserves in FX intervention, but credit and inter-bank markets have remained calm.

It is unlikely that any EM CB will react strongly to changes in the outlook for the Fed and US-yields before FX markets settle down. Once they do, faster and deeper Fed easing will open space for greater EM rate cuts. That is especially the case where cuts are warranted by domestic conditions but have been held back on fears that a narrowing of the interestrate differential versus the US could trigger FX weakness and financial instability. In many EMs, the cuts are now likely to be more front-loaded and in some terminal rates will also likely be lower than previously envisaged. But there is no one size that fits all. Domestic political and policy risks will weigh on the path of rate cuts, as will starting points relative to a neutral policy stance. In addition, the risk from a change in US trade, immigration, fiscal, and foreign policies post the November elections is high and could materially alter growth inflation-capital flow dynamics in EM.

## EM reaction material but not everywhere

The case for more aggressive EM easing has always been there. In most of EM, GDP in level terms still trails its prepandemic path (Figure 2). Similarly, real policy rates remain restrictive, even after realized—and indeed projected—easing (Figure 3). But equally, the hesitancy to ease more aggressively has multiple sources: political and policy risks (e.g., Brazil, Mexico, and South Africa); fears of triggering FX and financial instability where the buffer against US rates is low (most of EM Asia); and structural rather than cyclical factors holding growth down (CEE).

Figure 2: GDP's deviation from pre-pandemic trend
![](images/610947e6463757eeefa5a31ee9b3a386e9d444d9625be679fc056fa53d16d8e3.jpg)

Figure 3: Real\* policy rate
![](images/ab551491fe608a9d738386ef34e18689b54fabd899f9b466d7279956762b6688.jpg)

The loosening of the Fed constraint particularly strengthens the case for easing in EM Asia where we have added 50bp of cuts in India and Indonesia, and 10bp in China (Table 1). Elsewhere in EM, the projected easing in South Africa has been brought forward—we now look for a 50bp cut in September compared to 25bp earlier—but have retained total easing in this cycle at 100bp. In LatAm, we have added 100bp and 150bp to cuts in Brazil and Mexico, respectively, but these largely reverse the easing that was removed amid FX depreciation pressures stemming from rising policy risks and a decline in the market pricing of Fed cuts earlier this year.
