## EM Asia: Most to gain

At the top of the list stand India and Indonesia where we have added 75bp and 50bp cuts. Our extant policy rate forecast for India already had more easing than market pricing. That view was predicated on the continued decline in core inflation over the last nine months (currently at its lowest since 2012 at 3.%oya) being a telltale sign of large and, perhaps, widening excess capacity, which the RBI could not ignore for long. The newly added cuts reflect a better monsoon than previously expected that will keep food and headline inflation also under wraps and progress made by the RBI in taming the rise in unsecured credit by regulatory means, which had emerged as a key financial stability concern. Thus, even as the RBI is expected to stay on hold this week, we have brought forward the easing slated for 2025-26 firmly into 2H24 and 1H25.

In Indonesia core inflation has trailed below 2% for a long time and there is ample slack, hence domestic conditions alone call for 125bp of cuts through mid-2025. Instead, FX weakness had forced Bank of Indonesia (BI) to hike in September 2023 and April 2204 and to tighten short-term market rates through the sale of central bank bills. A widening bondyield differential with US, to which BI has shown more sensitivity than to the Fed rate per se, should allow easing to start in 3Q rather than 4Q24 and open space for another 75bp of cuts across 4Q24 and 1Q25,. This would be 50bp more than previously expected. BI could also signal its intentions by lowering short-term market rates earlier.

Since the beginning of the year, China’s PBOC has faced a policy dilemma where inadequate domestic demand and deflation pressures called for rate cuts, but CNY depreciation pressures demanded stable interest rates. The repricing of the Fed rate outlook provides a better FX environment. In the past two weeks, CNY has appreciated 1.7% against the USD, and the PBOC has taken the opportunity to restart rate cuts in late July, including a 10bp cut in 7-day reverse repo and prime lending rates, a 20bp cut in the 1-year MLF rate, and reductions in deposit rates. We now expect PBOC to add to the easing by cutting policy rates another 10bp in 4Q followed by another similar-sized reduction in 1Q25 that was already penciled in. These cuts are aimed at lifting growth and mitigating deflationary pressures.

Despite a choppy quarterly start to the year, Korea is on course to grow 2.7% in 2024, benefitting from strong external demand, especially in tech. As such, excess capacity is low calling for a gradual normalization of monetary policy to neutral. At the same time, household leverage remains elevated and further justifies cautious easing. While we had previously thought a rate cut we had pencilled in for October could be delayed on these concerns. But the Fed repricing has increased the odds of such a cut . While we still anticipate a total of 75bp of cuts in this cycle, they are likely to be delivered two quarters earlier than we previously though, by 2Q25

## Latam: Adding cuts but mostly in 2025

The market has been pricing aggressive rate hikes in Brazil on concerns of fiscal slippage that could unanchor inflation expectations. This rise in policy risk had led to a falling currency, adding to inflation concerns. While these conditions do not provide room to ease, we had anticipated policy to remain on hold for a prolonged period. This was based on the view that with the outlook for inflation improving at the margin, and the current account deficit well contained and easily funded by FDI flows, any further increase in the already high real interest rates could damage growth more than bring any associated benefits of better financial stability. Easier global financial conditions open room for lower rates, but they also reduce the incentives for more prudent fiscal policy. Thus, we remain with the forecasts for rates to be on hold for the time being. We expect the government to deliver a 2025 budget broadly consistent with the limits imposed by the fiscal framework, and also implement other needed fiscal measures. Once the fiscal policy risk is demonstratively dampened, the room for easing will likely open up and we pencil in a 100 bp easing starting in next June, taking the end-2025 policy rate to 9.5%.

Growth risks have shifted to the downside in Mexico as domestic demand has slowed appreciably in 2Q24. Some of the slowdown has been offset by a better net trade balance, but a marked real exchange appreciation continues to to buoy import growth. The recent depreciation of the nominal exchange rate, reflecting policy concerns post the general elections and then the unwinding of the yen carry trade, has helped to dampen the real exchange rate modestly. The abrupt depreciation has kept Banxico from easing more aggressively despite growth concerns, continued high real interest rates, and an appreciated real exchange rate. However, with the government already signalling a tight fiscal stance in 2025, the added Fed easing allows more breathing room for Banxico. We now expect Banxico to ease the policy rate to 10.25% this year and to 7.25% by end-2025. This will lower the real policy rate to around 3.7%, close but still above neutral, and help engineer a more gradual pace of real exchange rate depreciation to support exports, dampen import growth further, and better balance the domestic and external sectors of the economy.

## Emerging Europe: Modest changes

A key beneficiary of the deeper Fed cutting cycle is likely to be South Africa. The likelihood of a larger than our previously forecasted 25bp cut in September has now risen to near 50%, although FX pressures and services inflation will determine the eventuality. Moreover, we now expect a return to a neutral stance already by 1Q25 (previously mid-25). Even as political and fiscal risks have declined in recent months, the SARB has remained hesitant to start easing as inflation risks were skewed to the upside. With a material drop in services inflation recently, headline inflation now undershooting its target, and activity soft, the monetary policy stance is about 100bp tighter than warranted by growth-inflation dynamics. We think more front-loaded easing by the Fed on its own is not sufficient to alter the rate outlook. However, we believe core inflation may further surprise the SARB to the downside in the near-term. Provided that USDZAR does not depreciate much above 19 and the terms-of-trade remains steady, it now becomes a close call for a 50 versus 25bp cut in September or November.

CE4 central banks remain focused on inflation. Their success has been mixed: Czechia and Poland are near CB targets, while Hungary and Romania have still some distance to go. After the post-pandemic inflation shock that forced large interest rate hikes, CE4 central banks are less focused on supporting growth than managing inflation. With services inflation stubbornly sticky, headline inflation will likely remain high relative to target, limiting further material easing. More Fed easing is unlikely to change this calculus and, consequently, we keep our policy rate forecasts unchanged.

In contrast to the CE4, rates in Israel have traditionally been quite sensitive to US rates. However, given the very high geopolitical tensions in the region and increased risk premia in Israeli assets, we think the BoI is unlikely to follow the Fed closely this time around. While the chances of an earlier (October) re-start of the easing cycle in Israel have increased, we stick to our base case that the next 25bp cut will be delivered in November. That said, more space has been opened in 2025. We are adding one more cut to our forecast and now see the policy rate at 3.75% by mid-25 vs. 4.0% previously with risks skewed toward more cuts next year.

## Staying cautiously optimistic

These changes to our EM rate forecasts are clearly not as dramatic as those to the Fed call. While real interest rates are lowered modestly from the previous iteration, they remain not just high by pre-pandemic standards but also relative to the continued excess capacity in most economies (barring Latam driven largely by Brazil; Figure 4). This likely reflects the reemergence of inflation as the dominant concern of EM central banks, one which appeared to have eased in the years immediately prior to the pandemic. The rise in inflation concern is more obvious in economies such as Brazil and the CE-4 where the post-pandemic price increases were more painful than in EM Asia.

Figure 4: Real\* policy rate: EM ex Chn, Rus & Tur %p.a.; dotted line shows revised JPM forecast
![](images/d1601b0f053825f8ee1b3a14a71da030b294c6b60183526b1290a966326ff50f.jpg)

Caution on rate policy also reflects increased uncertainty around measures of potential growth (and thus output gaps) following the pandemic disruptions. When such uncertainties rise, central banks naturally put less weight on indicators of slack in line with prescriptions from optimal control theory.

In addition, while the Fed call is comparatively less affected by US elections, it is not so for EM monetary policy. EM growth and financial conditions can be severely affected by changes in US trade, immigration, and foreign policies. The risk from that source is likely to weigh heavily in the considerations of EM central banks in the coming months. This also underlies our hesitancy in forecasting more front-loaded EM easing to match what we are expecting from the Fed.

In any event, policy rates remain restrictive and well above neutral rates even at end-2025. At the same time, rate differentials with the Fed have widened in favor of EM providing more assurance of financial stability (Figure 5). Consequently, if EM central banks get greater clarity on how much excess capacity there truly is in their economies (e.g., through a continued decline in core inflation) and the US election risk does not materialize, there may be significantly more easing in 2025 than we have penciled in.

Figure 5: Policy rate differential vs the Fed: EM ex Chn, Rus & Tur %p.a.; dotted line shows rate differential with revised JPM forecasts
![](images/45c4614d660f8033bc24bd9bc187e5e61424f064f2b5d5cd4d79cfa61494efd8.jpg)

## US: The when and why of intermeeting Fed rate cuts

• Following the weaker July jobs report, Fed policy looks increasingly offsides

We look for an accelerated and front-loaded easing cycle, with 50bp cuts in September and November

Historically, a big financial market shock or recessionary signals needed to trigger an intermeeting move

Following the July jobs report, the Fed’s policy stance is looking increasingly offsides: with core PCE inflation cooling and the unemployment rate rising to 4.3% last month, a Taylor Rule would suggest a fed funds rate target around 4%—150bp below the current level. Given the shift in the balance of risks that Chair Powell highlighted at last week’s FOMC, we see a case for quickly bringing policy more in alignment with the economy. Markets have responded by pricing over 100bp of rate cuts by year-end. We expect 125bp of easing this year, with front-loaded 50bp moves at each of the next two FOMC meetings. An intermeeting cut, meanwhile, is currently a low risk: historically, the trigger for such a move would likely be either signs of significant financial instability or further weakness in incoming economic data that points to rising recessionary concerns.

## A brief history of intermeeting moves

Since the Fed began its modern approach to communications in 1994, which included publishing official statements after FOMC meetings and communicating its policy moves rather than leaving markets guessing, there have been eight intermeeting policy rate cuts—and one hike, in 1994 (Table 1).

Table 1: History of intermeeting Fed policy moves
<table><tr><td>Date</td><td>Move</td><td>Rationale</td><td>Context</td></tr><tr><td>18-Apr-94</td><td>+25bp</td><td>Lagged start to hiking cycle</td><td>Pace of hiking accelerated afterwards</td></tr><tr><td>15-Oct-98</td><td>-25bp</td><td>Russia crisis, LTCM failure</td><td>Middle of brief easing cycle</td></tr><tr><td>3-Jan-01</td><td>-50bp</td><td>Dotcom bubble aftermath</td><td>Start of easing cycle</td></tr><tr><td>18-Apr-01</td><td>-50bp</td><td>Preempt economic weakness</td><td>Early- to mid-easing cycle</td></tr><tr><td>17-Sep-01</td><td>-50bp</td><td>9/11 response</td><td>Reaccelerated easing pace</td></tr><tr><td>22-Jan-08</td><td>-75bp</td><td>Housing bubble aftermath</td><td>Accelerated easing pace</td></tr><tr><td>8-Oct-08</td><td>-50bp</td><td>Global financial crisis</td><td>Reaccelerated easing pace</td></tr><tr><td>3-Mar-20</td><td>-50bp</td><td>Pandemic response</td><td>Start of easing cycle</td></tr><tr><td>15-Mar-20</td><td>-100bp</td><td>Pandemic response</td><td>Cut rates to ELB</td></tr></table>

Source: Federal Reserve, J.P. Morgan

Other intermeeting policy changes included a discount window cut in August 2007 as the housing market collapse began, the introduction of a range of balance sheet programs following the Global Financial Crisis in 2008 and the pandemic in 2020, and the oft-cited grandaddy of them all: the Greenspan Fed flooding the market with liquidity in October 1987 after Black Monday.

As Table 1 indicates, intermeeting moves typically are in response to significant dislocations in financial markets (1998, 2001, 2008) or a material change in the economic outlook (1994, 2001, 2020). They have occasionally marked the start of an easing cycle (January 2001, March 2020), but more often have signaled an acceleration (or occasionally a re-acceleration) of the pace of rate cuts. They also tend to come in bunches: three in 2001, two each in 2008 and in 2020.

## History doesn’t repeat, but does rhyme

The prior intermeeting move most similar to now may be January 2001. That 50bp cut began an extended easing cycle, and while the bursting dot-com equity bubble was the proximate cause then, the focus like now was on the economy—specifically a shift from concern about elevated inflation at the December 2000 FOMC meeting to economic weakness in early January. The 2001 easing cycle featured 11 cuts (including three intermeeting moves, each of 50bp) that took the funds rate from 6.5% at the start of the year to 1.75% by the end (Figure 1). That said, the policy rate stood at 3.5% prior to the terrorist attacks on September 11. The FOMC responded to that shock with a 50bp intermeeting ease on September 17, followed by three more cuts of cumulatively 125bp.

Figure 1: FOMC fed funds rate target
Percent; dashed lines indicate dates of intermeeting moves
![](images/e3153c7165dda69929df154e4f190f20b7f947001d1953687718a86d901364f6.jpg)

The other move most similar to today, ironically, may be the April 1994 hike. Then, the Fed came to the realization it had dragged its feet on changing the policy cycle (in that case, to tighten), and moved somewhat earnestly thereafter. While it did not kick off the tightening cycle (there were two prior 25bp hikes in 1994), the hiking pace subsequently accelerated into early 1995, with one 75bp and three 50bp hikes.

## Putting the put back in place?

Only a few Fed officials have spoken since the July jobs report—and none of the leadership—but remarks from regional Fed presidents Goolsbee (Chicago) and Daly (San Francisco) have not indicated support for an intermeeting move. That’s not surprising; at this point, we think the Fed would need to see either a sharp sell-off in financial markets or signs of a looming recession in the incoming data. Despite widespread belief in the “Fed put” among market participants (due in no small part to Greenspan’s intermeeting moves in 1987 and 1998), the current FOMC seems far more likely to respond to signs of vanishing liquidity and contracting credit availability than to an equity market correction alone.

Indeed, many of the previous intermeeting eases coincide with a sizable deterioration in broad financial conditions (e.g., the Chicago Fed’s National Financial Conditions Index) and/ or a jump in financial stress (based on a measure compiled by the US Treasury’s Office of Financial Research). Figure 2 indicates past disruptions to financial markets that have been associated with an intermeeting Fed response. While there has been a moderate jump in the Treasury’s FSI measure recently, the level is still well below prior episodes of significant dislocations. Specifically, while the volatility component of the index spiked the Monday after the July jobs report, it gave back a sizable portion of that jump the next day. Additionally, the credit component had a much smaller rise while the liquidity piece saw no meaningful change. It thus would take a much greater intensification of financial instability in the near term could bring the FOMC to consider an intermeeting move.

Figure 2: Fed intermeeting moves and financial conditions Index, both axes. Dashed lines indicate dates of intermeeting moves
![](images/477c267cb29951a5ba49e970f52e8fb1c4c3dea40b1765a9555f1ca4d1066171.jpg)

Recent declines in longer-dated interest rates may reflect market concerns about recession in addition to expectations for a more aggressively front-loaded easing cycle. In a recent speech, Daly (who is a voter on the FOMC this year) argued for the latter interpretation: she proposed that markets understand the Fed’s reaction function and thus recent price action—namely lower interest rates on mortgages, autos, and other consumer loans—anticipates upcoming policy easing. She concluded, “you already see policy working, even before we cut the rate.” If this view is shared more widely on the FOMC, it would also reduce the odds of an intermeeting cut.

## The economic case for an early ease

While greater volatility in financial markets cannot be ruled out, we think the more likely scenario to deliver an intermeeting rate cut would be additional weakening in upcoming economic data releases—although that is not our base case. Further evidence of a deterioration in the labor market would be the most likely trigger, and that would likely require a persistent further spike up in jobless claims. Another possible trigger would be signs of a contraction in the activity data later this month, such as retail sales, industrial production, or consumer spending—the same indicators that the NBER monitors to determine if the economy has slid into a recession. Of course, another weak jobs report on September 6 could also make the case to immediately get the easing cycle underway. That timing wouldn’t be completely unprecedented, either: the January 2008 intermeeting cut was just eight days before the next scheduled FOMC meeting, while the moves in September 2001 and October 1987 were 15 and 16 days prior, respectively.

The idea that the policy rate is significantly offsides right now is reinforced by looking at Taylor Rule estimates of the “appropriate” policy stance given current values of core PCE inflation and the unemployment rate. Figure 3 shows the gap between the funds rate target and a standard Taylor Rule estimate<sup>1</sup>for the months of each of the intermeeting moves in Table 1. The sign of the gap matches that of the intermeeting move in all cases expect March 2020, although that is a timing issue: the unemployment rate surge the following month suggests a negative Taylor rule estimate, and the FOMC cut a total of 150bp across three weeks in anticipation. For July of this year, the gap is just over 150bp, larger than any prior deviation at the time of an intermeeting policy move, although we don’t expect the Powell Fed to opt for an intermeeting cut unless unavoidable.

Figure 3: Fed funds rate gap to Taylor rule
Bp deviation between Taylor rule (1993) and funds rate target
![](images/1b72bfd21aa9c1ab4fdd00ac67fda72d25ecb7e5c7bafa1f9644909a7f2e05df.jpg)
Source: Federal Reserve, J.P. Morgan

## US: Peering through the choppiness in claims

• Summer increase in initial claims may be partly residual seasonality, though evidence is mixed

• After accounting for some one-off shocks, summer claims aren’t significantly higher than recent years

Rising continuing claims are a source of concern, even if they haven’t risen much relative to the unemployed

With the unemployment rate rising 0.2%-pt in July, and almost a full percentage point since the 2023 low, there is heightened risk that the economy could further deteriorate in the near term. It is thus useful to leverage the timely signal coming from weekly unemployment insurance claims to track the evolution of the labor market. Both initial and continuing jobless claims have recently increased, suggesting some softness, with the four-week average of initial claims rising by about 25k since March/April. However, the message is muddied because there was a similarly timed rise in initial claims last year that was not sustained (Figure 1), raising the question of whether this year’s increase is simply residual seasonality.

Figure 1: Weekly initial jobless claims, four-week average
![](images/253a70830ee892122587c9098b450dfd5cd583355ee2d234a93bf1f90f4ace60.jpg)
Source: Department of Labor, J.P. Morgan

We find that in 2023 initial claims were boosted by a set of factors that are largely not repeating this year, and while there may be some residual seasonality that drove up claims in May and June the evidence isn’t clear cut. At the same time, unusually low claims in the winter months in both 2023 and 2024 mean that the rise in seasonally adjusted claims since the start of the year is larger than it would have been had winter claims been at more normal levels. This could mean that seasonally adjusted claims will fall again this winter even if labor market conditions are stable.

Finally, the rise in continuing claims that began in June is suggestive of softer labor conditions, but when viewed over the last year the increase is still small in comparison to rising unemployment in the monthly household survey. Comparing to absolute growth in job losers in the household survey may be unreliable, but it is notable that the ratio of continuing claims to payroll employment is about flat over the last year, while the ratio of unemployed permanent job losers to the labor force or household employment is up about 20%. This subject will require further investigation.

## Mixed evidence for residual seasonality

Residual seasonality typically occurs either when seasonal factors change in an unusual way that does not correspond to the pattern in the not seasonally adjusted (NSA) data, or when seasonal factors are stable but year after year these factors fail to fully adjust for a seasonal pattern. The first situation occurred last year in continuing claims, a problem that disappeared when revised seasonal factors were released this March (we previously wrote about this issue here).

The seasonal factor this year is not that different from past years, which can be seen by re-calculating seasonally adjusted claims using seasonal factors from prior years with the same calendar (Figure 1). We leverage seasonal factors from 2013 and 2019 for this and use the factors as they were reported in early 2020, so that they are not effected by COVID data or a new seasonal adjustment model that the Department of Labor began using this spring. Using the older factors, the level of claims would have been a bit higher in March and April, but the change from either the end of April or the beginning of the year is basically identical (Figure 2).

Figure 2: 4W avg of initial claims, using unrevised historical SF
![](images/14fa554531701a796c3023333fb2b244e15beac0947407307b7f13f58063cf5e.jpg)

The evidence is more mixed as to whether claims consistently rise in the summer even after seasonal adjustment. While only the last five years use seasonal factors from the new DoL model, the prior paragraph highlighted that factors haven’t changed that much over time, so looking at patterns across many years should still be useful. Because comparisons are difficult with weekly data we look at the change in the monthly average for May and June, as this is when claims increased in 2023 and 2024. Given that claims often decline for many years during economic recoveries we de-trend the series with a 6m centered moving average, though results are similar when using a 12m window or using trailing instead of centered averages (though de-trending with long trailing averages tend to make May and June changes look large if they happen to occur at a turning point in claims, as was the case in 2018 and 2022).

The results show that since 2017 claims have tended to rise in May and June, though there is no pattern before that (Figure 3). The change in 2017 onward is strictly positive for any of the types of de-trending we use and also without any detrending, and if this is a new pattern then at least some of the increase in 2024 may simply be residual seasonality, though it is hard to know for sure with a short sample and a change in seasonal model.

Figure 3: Monthly average change in weekly claims, May & June 000s, sa
![](images/e35568b276956afe365b3e7240099747adae8479c79817b046628c5653023391.jpg)

## Nuts and bolts of 2023/24

Given the uncertainty as to whether there is residual seasonality, it is useful to simply compare not seasonally adjusted claims in 2024 to earlier years (Figure 4). Two points stand out. First, from roughly May to September of 2023 the level of claims was was more elevated than earlier years, consistent with the spike in summer seasonally adjusted claims that year. Second, claims had been more subdued this year, but have recently moved more in line with 2023 and are above earlieryear levels.

Figure 4: Not seasonally adjusted initial jobless claims, by week #
![](images/c72ad39fb163c4b9b6b2d79d9f5dc44c51d8d5789ffbde4bd067697e4534ada6.jpg)

However, some of the increase in 2023 and 2024 relative to earlier years reflects technical factors. In particular, in 2023 there were unusual mid-year increases in claims in Ohio and Texas that did not translate into first payments, which could be attempted fraud, or some other factor that led unqualified claimants to apply in unusually large numbers. In 2024 these spikes did not appear, but Texas had elevated claims for much of July stemming from Hurricane Beryl. Finally, in May 2023 Minnesota extended unemployment insurance benefits to non-instructional education support professionals for the first time, which will lift NSA claims every summer going forward, particularly in June. In 2024 this program likely boosted the monthly average of seasonally adjusted claims for June by about 4,000, and around half as much in 2023.

Figure 5: Not seasonally adjusted initial jobless claims, by week # 000s, nsa, 4w average
![](images/a8f0bdd85fdd323f342a2c6dcf69defc378032e61daa47aef3c1779fd8b05997.jpg)

Excluding these three states, the level of claims during the summer of 2023 looks less unusual (Figure 5), though August and September were still somewhat elevated. Nonetheless, seasonally adjusted claims would still have increased, albeit less significantly (Figure 6). This pattern is repeated this year, though the upward drift in claims excluding Ohio, Texas, and Minnesota has became slightly more pronounced this year than last year, and the four-week average in the latest week is between 10k and 17k higher than earlier years. This in part reflects a surge in claims in Michigan, which could just indicate that summer auto plant shutdowns were unusually elevated this year. According to estimates from S&P Global, Michigan auto production was down 31%oya in July, a larger annual decline than other recent months. In the latest weekly reading claims in Michigan have mostly normalized, which will flow into the four-week average in coming weeks.

Figure 6: Seasonally adjusted claims, four-week avg, by week # Percent of full-year average
![](images/d7e7544ced58e08bf019d2c0d7bf519eb7686ed78ac5264f887e7e6abbb0cc96.jpg)

Moreover, the increase since the start of the year is not so much because claims are exceptionally elevated during the summer, but rather that they have been low in the winter. The 2023 and 2024 seasonal factors expected claims in January and February to be 16% higher than the full-year average, but 2023 actual figures were only 10% greater, and the 2024 levels were similar to 2023. To put that in perspective, suppose the January and February values for NSA claims in 2023 and 2024 were set equal to the more elevated 2019 value. Then seasonally adjusted claims would still be lowest during the spring and fall, but the summer value in 2023 (excluding Ohio, Texas, and Minnesota) would be lower than in the winter, and the summer value in 2024 would be only marginally higher.

Why have initial claims been lower in the winter in recent years? Regionally the effect is distributed across a number of states, and industry data on initial claims is not readily accessible at a national level. In addition, while continuing claims are available by industry once a month and were low in the winter for 2023, they were not low in 2024, so it is not clear they can be used to fully explain the low winter values for initial claims. However, a possible explanation is that outdoor industries sensitive to cold weather like construction and agriculture—which do have consistently lower continuing claims in both 2023 and 2024—are driving the change.

If initial claims do repeat last year’s pattern and are unusually low from December on then seasonally adjusted claims could fall significantly: last December the four-week average of claims ended the year at 206k. Even absent low winter readings, if claims were simply to track last year’s pattern through the fall the four-week average could fall under 220k by October. This is not unfathomable since as shown above claims are still not materially higher than earlier years. Of course such an outcome is not assured if the economy weakens. From now until the end of September it will be easiest to track claims by comparing them to earlier years excluding Ohio, Texas, and Minnesota. Thereafter it should be possible to start simply comparing claims across all states again.

# UK housing reform: Aiming high but expecting low

Labour government intends to use reforms to help deliver 1.5m new homes over the next five years

This could add up to 0.3%-pts to annual growth, but the target is ambitious and probably unlikely to be met

More realistically, we think the reforms could result in a direct growth impact closer to 0.1%-pt per year

The new Labour government intends to boost growth by using reforms to incentivise private sector investment, and home building has become the flagship of that strategy. We look at the likelihood of success through a relaxation of planning restrictions, and assess the potential growth impact. We haven’t changed our relatively pessimistic view on these kind of policies (see “An uphill struggle for Labour in Government”) and still think the government will struggle to achieve its target to build 1.5m homes by 2029.

If the target is fully met, the direct impact could add up to 0.3%-pt to annual growth. This growth contribution is not without precedent but would likely require favourable macro factors including lower rates and a broader economic upswing. Based on policy alone, a slower improvement in home building and to a lower level would more realistically lift average growth by closer to 0.1%. To successfully lift trend growth, Labour would have to rely on a much broader and as yet mostly unspecified set of reforms. This is why we expect the government to become frustrated over time and find ways to boost public sector investment (see “Exploring Labour’s subtle Plan B”).

Figure 1: UK housing supply
![](images/2ba55a53d75a8f8eaf2795b77a45c7c129d0807d70b5467b0d7db7f5e1bd613f.jpg)

## Challenges in meeting the target

In her first speech as Chancellor, Rachel Reeves vowed to deliver “one and a half million homes over the next five years”. This works out to be 300,000 new homes per year during the length of the current five-year parliament. At first glance, this looks unachievable. A simple inspection of the long standing data on new home completions shows that the required number of new builds hasn’t been seen since the 1970s (Figure 1, blue line) with the latest data closer to 180k. Back then, completions by social housing developers were running at close to 150k a year and responsible for around half of the total increase (Figure 2). For several years now, social housing has been running at an annual pace of closer to 40k. Moreover, there was a boom in home building by private sector enterprises that began after WWII and continued into the 1970s. Recent timely data is limited and sometimes only available for England, but we have estimated a figure for the UK, while making a projection for 2024 based on recent data. At present, private sector work is some 30% lower than the peak seen during the 1960s and early 1970s.

Figure 2: Housing completions in the UK, old methodology
![](images/38073c8e3a671f3d1792434daad70f062ef6050c7ac1057901a1d99966fd1622.jpg)

All this being said, the government’s target does still look attainable if a different definition is used. The Department for Levelling up, Housing and Communities (DLUHC) prefers a relatively new but broader measure of home building. This uses a more comprehensive data set that incorporates information on site visits, council tax records, planning databases and building control records. This is already running higher to begin with, at above 230k (Figure 1, green line).

Moreover, there are ways of boosting home supply without actually building anything new. This is the case when existing properties have either been (a) converted from a house into several flats or (b) converted into homes from space previously used for commercial purposes. These elements are small but still make a difference of around 20k or so per year to result in an even higher recent rate of 250k (Figure 1, orange line). This broader measure is sometimes referred to as “Net Supply” or “Net additional dwellings”.

Based on this definition, the government’s target appears easier to reach. Indeed, the data over the more recent five-year period 2018-2022 show home additions averaging over 280k with a peak of almost 300k in 2019. But there remains a big challenge, as the very latest data through 1Q24 show declining momentum. Even a rise to 300k would require a proportionate 25% increase. This is unlikely to happen in the space of just one year. Given the lower starting point, the only way to meet the required five year average pace of new home creation would be to deliver a higher total of closer to 340k by 2029. This would be an increase of 40% from current levels and does look very difficult without public investment.

Only limited details have so far been provided with regards to changes to the planning system, and projects clearly need to be planned some time before building begins. Some have also questioned whether there is the capacity within the private sector to produce a rapid increase in short order. The level of residential investment does look particularly low, compared not only to other forms of investment but also its own history (Figure 3). That may offer greater scope for a large rise, but this would probably only happen during a broader cyclical upswing in the economy, as seen from 2010 to 2019.

Figure 3: Gross fixed capital formation
![](images/bb885eb71642f704ffee3a8d5693802f8f0bf905e7bfda848211aeb7f76ee096.jpg)

Figure 4: Housing completions and residential investment
![](images/ab31e3403f100eb6dae2748e5e1dd9bc65254ced2d01cb1a0af73d03689c096e.jpg)

## A modest plausible growth impact

If the government’s target is met, what would the growth impact be? There is a fairly reliable relationship between new housing completions and residential investment (Figure 4). Based on the analysis above, i.e. allowing for other sources of new home additions and the current low starting level, we estimate new home completions (i.e. new builds) would need to rise to around 300k over the next five years to achieve the government’s target. This would be a 40% rise from our projection for 2024, and would likely produce a similar rise in residential investment. Residential investment accounts for a quarter of overall gross fixed investment in the UK, and around 5% of GDP. A 40% rise in residential investment would hence add around 1.6% to the level of GDP. Spread over a five-year period, the direct impact of this would be to lift growth by around 0.3%-pts each year (Table 1).

Table 1: Estimated impact of planning reforms on GDP growth
<table><tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td colspan="2">Housing completions</td><td colspan="2">Residential investment</td><td colspan="3">GDP contribution</td></tr><tr><td>000s</td><td>%ch</td><td>Level, £bn</td><td>%ch</td><td>Total</td><td>5 yr avg.</td><td>10yr. avg.</td></tr><tr><td>2024 forecast</td><td>216</td><td>0</td><td>95.1</td><td>0</td><td>0.0</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Scenarios</td><td>225</td><td>4</td><td>99.1</td><td>4</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>250</td><td>16</td><td>110.1</td><td>16</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>275</td><td>27</td><td>121.1</td><td>27</td><td>1.1</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>300</td><td>39</td><td>132.1</td><td>39</td><td>1.6</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>350</td><td>62</td><td>154.2</td><td>62</td><td>2.6</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.3</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan calculations. Refers to new builds under the DLUHC's net additional dwel ings measure

This is not insignificant for an economy that appears to have experienced trend growth no better than 1% in recent years, but the risk is that the government’s housing reforms take longer to have an effect or will simply fall short of the target. For example, under a scenario where new builds rose 275k and the adjustment took twice as long, the annual growth impact would be closer to 0.1%. This would be small compared to the impact that other factors can have on the sector, including interest rate rises over the past couple of years and broader economic conditions (Figure 3).

## Watching the PMI to track the upswing

We estimate the government’s target would require average annual gains in new home additions of around 7% a year. Given reporting lags in the official data, the timeliest means of gauging whether Labour’s housing targets are on track is to look at the housing construction reading of the monthly PMI. To hit an annual growth rate of 7%, the housing construction PMI would need to rise to the high 50s. At present, it has recovered from its 2023 low of 38, but is sitting only a little above 50 (Figure 5). The required growth rates of private sector activity have been seen in the past, but often only during the recovery phase following recessions.

Figure 5: PMI housing contruction and net home additions
![](images/02c5368ab8128dc5f48cffbe3fce0f0bbee4dc86b64fce91e4deff2a82c3b0f2.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, ONS

## Australian wages: Herd mentality

Wage growth has now peaked, with the annual rate to move gradually lower as the jobless rate increases

• Lags mean wages set via collective agreements are likely to outperform in the near term...

• ...while the impulse from individual arrangements, awards and the minimum wage fades

• We expect 2Q wage growth will print at 0.8%q/q sa in next week’s release

Last week’s 2Q CPI data in Australia were relatively well behaved, with both headline and trimmed mean inflation printing broadly in line with the RBA’s forecast. Inflation has been the obvious and dominant driver of the rate cycle, but as we have noted previously the RBA follows a dual mandate with labor market performance also an important input in the policy mix. On this front, the next data point of note is the upcoming 2Q wage price index (WPI) which will provide an important update on underlying inflation pressure coming from the labor market. This week the RBA reiterated its expectation that wage growth has now peaked and will moderate from current levels, a view broadly aligned with the J.P. Morgan outlook. Here we take a closer look at medium-term wage dynamics, but with respect to next week’s data, we think wages increased 0.8%q/q in the June quarter, with the annual rate slowing two-tenths to 3.9%oya.

Figure 1: Share of wage growth explained by the unemployment rate
![](images/d7be6b4c33b9085bb6d333b9ccb8a753a54f3df149b438fb2e2637d1c96e5d27.jpg)
Source: ABS, J.P. Morgan

## Understanding the mechanism

Australian wages are predominantly set via individual arrangement (39% of total), collective agreement (34%) or the legislated award/minimum (23%). Individual arrangements capture specific wage negotiations between private parties, are generally more flexible than other wage setting methods and, in certain cases, may be reviewed intra-year. In contrast, collective agreements and award wages are reviewed periodically with terms, conditions and pay rates valid for the duration of the agreement, meaning wages set under these

methods maintain a relatively low beta to real-time labor market developments. This dynamic is evident when regressing the growth rate of the different mechanisms against the jobless rate (Figure 1), with contemporaneous unemployment changes most influential for individual arrangements, ahead of collective agreements and legislated awards.

## Group think

The most recent tracking data show wages set via collective agreement increased, on average, a shade under 4% in the June quarter, above spot inflation (3.8%oya) and annualized WCI growth (1Q24: 3.6% saar). The relative strength in collective agreements is not surprising given renewed contracts reflect not just prevailing economic conditions, but also incorporate developments and related wage outcomes since the prior agreement was updated. That is a considerable time horizon given the average contract spans 36 months. Accordingly, our expectation is that prior strength in aggregate wage growth and inflation will continue to support collective agreement wage rates even as labor demand slows.There is tentative evidence such a dynamic is already at play with the contribution of individual arrangements and collective agreements to aggregate wage growth converging in the past year (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Contribution to aggregate wage growth
![](images/e454afd188b72f67f6be2a203c03b19d25d9b1971f336c128c3d897e748efa24.jpg)

We’ve previously noted that the prevalence of collective agreements/awards has the effect of smoothing and elongating the broader wage cycle. This is now apparent in the indus try-level data where the relationship between WCI growth and the share of industry wages set by collective agreements has reversed and turned positive (Figure 3). Indeed, tight labor market conditions from 2H22 delivered relatively swift wage growth to sectors dominated by individual arrangement (professional, wholesale), while sectors reliant on longer-term wage contracts (education, healthcare) reported modest growth. As of 1Q24, these trends have shifted with healthcare and education wages now outperforming, while the deterioration in labor market conditions has contributed to weaker wage outcomes in those sectors linked to individual contracts.

Figure 3: Wage growth vs share of collective agreements Wages, %oya
![](images/681bc4ead860299e3011056e03682447adaaae18668735c48ba7b16f9a8ee78e.jpg)

## Can’t fight the fundamentals

While wage growth in collective agreements is likely to stay strong, we think industries where wages are mostly determined by individual arrangements and private negotiations will experience a more pronounced slowdown as labor demand cools and unemployment increases. To highlight different industries’ sensitivity to changes in underlying labor conditions, we use a vector auto regression (VAR) to model industry wage growth as a function of the unemployment rate and annual real GDP growth. As expected, sectors with the largest share of individual arrangements (professional, wholesale) are generally more sensitive to permanent unemployment rate shocks, while industries where wages are set via collective agreements (education, health) have a lower beta to contemporaneous unemployment outcomes.

Figure 4: Response of wage growth to unemployment shock %, annual wage response following 1% increase in unemployment rate
![](images/927f5d7d05d5eb4bc26b9fbd76e28e9e668b058d37f5a1231b06efdb0f1694f0.jpg)

Our expectation is for the jobless rate to gradually drift higher and approach the mid-4s by year-end. This view is premised on lagged effects from policy tightening delivered earlier in the cycle and the outperformance of the unemployment rate relative to domestic demand growth, with the current wedge between these two series large by historical standards (Figure 5). While recent developments (for example, working from home, labor hoarding, migration growth) may have influenced the beta between these series, we regard the level of unemployment as unsustainable and expect a partial realignment over time. There is evidence this is already occurring with the jobless rate now 0.6%-pts above the cycle low, while the more reliable leading labor indicators (NAB survey, job vacancies) suggest labor demand will continue to cool.

Figure 5: Unemployment rate and domestic demand
![](images/a3ce000dacfd3fa8c75f284a04f60d3a9ea011583f31516ced7d6aee3409af17.jpg)

## Legislative wage pressure cooling

Australia’s award and minimum wage is reviewed annually by the Fair Work Commission (FWC) with changes to wage rates implemented from 1 July. As a result the announcement earlier this year will only become evident in the data from the September quarter, while the coming week’s impulse will effectively amount to zero. With respect to the 3Q outcome, the FWC announced the award and minimum wage will increase 3.75%y/y in FY2025. This is below the average of the past two years (5.5%y/y) and the broader wage price index (4.1%oya), but still higher than the immediate pre-COVID average (3.3%y/y).

Award/minimum wage agreements are reset annually so legislative outcomes are routinely captured in the WPI release. Therefore ,what matters most for estimating the impulse to aggregate wage growth is strength/weakness versus the longer-run average. In our view, the FY2025 award/minimum wage decision will be neutral for 3Q wages and deliver a contribution very close to zero, significantly lower than last year’s outcome which lifted the WCI by \~0.5%-pts. Fading supports from legislated wage growth alongside our forecast for moderation in wages set via individual arrangements underpins our view that annual wage growth has already peaked, and will drift lower in coming quarters, with collective agreement wage growth to outperform the other mechanisms.

## United States

• New claims for unemployment insurance moved lower, though continuing claims increased again

Lending conditions still tight, and household loan performance deteriorated last quarter

• Service surveys send mixed, though not alarming, messages

• July CPI expected to remain tame and retail sales growth looks to be modest

Compared to last week’s event-filled calendar, the relatively quiet schedule this week gave time to pause and reflect on the state of the business cycle. Perhaps the most watched report was weekly jobless claims. In the end it was a glass half-full, half-empty release. Initial claims retraced much of the prior move up; however, continuing claims increased yet again, even with a likely decline in storm-related filings. Likewise, the NY Fed’s quarterly consumer credit report sent mixed messages. While overall delinquency rates aren’t particularly high, they continue to move up.

This normalization of both labor market conditions and household balance sheets was a theme in last week’s remarks from Fed chair Powell. Current conditions are generally favorable, but if the normalization (i.e., slowing) continues then this would present a problem. We still feel that, with the Fed’s employment and inflation risks about balanced, there is a good case to be made for rapidly getting back to neutral to better address emerging risks to either side of its mandate. Fed speakers this week didn’t shed much light on how they’re thinking about policy prospects, and it is likely that the next red-letter day for Fed talk won’t come until Powell’s Jackson Hole remarks in two weeks’ time.

The Fed stayed in the news toward the end of the week when Republican presidential nominee Trump said that the president should have a say in setting interest rate policy. In a recent webinar with Sarah Binder (link here), we discussed various ways the White House can exert influence on the Fed. While there are a number of institutional safeguards to preserve the Fed’s independence, it is possible the courts could reinterpret some of the statutes establishing those safeguards.

Next week will bring two important data points. On Wednesday, we expect the July CPI will print a low-side 0.2% on the ex-food and energy core measure. If realized, this would like ly give the Fed further confidence that inflation is settling in close enough to its 2% goal. On Thursday, we look for retail sales to increase 0.4% and the core “control” segment to edge up 0.1%. If realized, real consumer spending growth would be off to a modest start for the quarter.

## Mixed messages from claims

Initial jobless claims in the week ending August 3 declined to 233k from 250k the prior week, which had been the high for the year (Figure 1). The four-week averaged edged up to 241k, which is a high for this year though only a bit above where the average had been at the end of June. Claims had been unusually elevated recently in both Texas and Michigan, with Texas likely still recording some impact from Hurricane Beryl and Michigan possibly seeing more claims than usual from summer auto plant shutdowns. These earlier increases mostly normalized in the latest week, leaving the evolution of summer claims not significantly different than recent years.

![](images/d4a20702a689dc929125da44c814cab0f5302608e7b69809d5a40ddded83cf07.jpg)

While the Department of Labor doesn’t produce seasonally adjusted state estimates, adjusted values from Haver Analytics showed claims in those two states falling by 14k (note that the sum of changes in these seasonally adjusted state claims won’t sum to the total seasonally adjusted change reported by the Department of Labor, with the sum of seasonally adjusted states declining by a larger 25k).

Overall, the latest figure appears less influenced by distortions than earlier weeks. While claims are higher than in March/April, they haven’t moved much since June. In the context of recent concerns about the labor market that is good news, as it argues against any sudden weakening.

The message from continuing claims was less positive, as continuing claims in the week ending July 27 rose to 1.875mm from 1.869mm, with the latest reading a new high for this year. Continuing claims had started to increase materially in June but then stayed mostly stable for five weeks, excluding a spike last week that appeared to be driven by Hurricane Beryl claims in Texas. But continuing claims in Texas (and Michigan) pulled back this week, even as total claims hit their new high. The rise in continuing claims over the last couple months could point to heightened challenges in the ability of laid-off workers to find new jobs.

## Service surveys go their separate ways

The latest service sector surveys from S&P Global and ISM continue to portray different trends through July, with the services PMI not pointing to any break in the economic growth trend of recent quarters while the ISM suggests a gradual cooling in the pace of growth. This is despite the fact that the services PMI fell in the final July report (55.0) compared to the earlier flash survey (56.0) and June (55.3), while the ISM rebounded after a particularly weak June, with the composite rising to 51.4 from 48.8 and the business activity index improving to 54.5 from 49.6 (Figure 2).

FIgure 2: Service sector new orders/business measures
![](images/e0746fcfefe951e966ced797666267dfc39445356b5d6e6f4db77e54bc0f878b.jpg)

The business activity indexes in the two reports are now at almost identical levels, but because the services PMI has typically run weaker than the ISM, the PMI figure is better in comparison to its own history. Moreover, the PMI’s general business activity and new business readings have on average improved over recent months despite the dip in July, while the equivalent measures in the ISM report have been trending down even if they did recover some last month.

## Lending conditions still a headwind

The Fed’s latest Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey, which tracks changes in bank lending standards and loan demand over the three-month period that mostly overlaps with the second quarter, reported a positive net fraction of banks still tightening lending standards, though the share continued to decline against prior quarters. In the past the start of a recession has usually corresponded with a period when the pace of tightening was accelerating, with that pace peaking either around the start of the recession or in the midst of it. In this cycle, a tightening in standards began with the Fed rate-rise cycle, and the pace of tightening peaked back in mid- to late-2023, just after the regional banking crisis. That in turn has contributed to a slowdown in the expansion of bank lending, with real loan balances at commercial banks roughly unchanged in the year to June.

FIgure 3: Domestic banks tightening C&I loan standards Net %
![](images/58642270a7be671ec0329932583e95afd48a0f0c7aaf7f5eff828bbf2b6a2fd3.jpg)

The survey reports changes in lending standards for C&I, CRE, residential real estate, and other consumer loans. For C&I loans, the net fraction of domestic banks tightening standards for large and medium firms eased to 7.9% from 15.6% the prior quarter and a peak of 50.8% in 3Q23, while the net share reporting stronger demand jumped from -26.6% to 0% (Figure 3). Likewise, for commercial real estate the net share of banks tightening standards fell to 22.2% from 29.7% the prior quarter and the 2Q23 peak of 68.3%, while net demand rose to -17.5% from -26.6%. (The Fed actually publishes results only for individual types of CRE loans, and we average across those here; the same approach is used for residential mortgages.) The net fraction of banks reporting stronger demand is weakest for CRE, with residential real estate only slightly better (-14.8%). This is not surprising given concerns about the health of some property types in the CRE market, along with the drag from higher rates on both commercial and residential real estate lending.

## Fed balance sheet back in focus

Two developments have brought back questions about when the Fed may halt its balance sheet reduction plan. First, the prospect of accelerated rate cuts has prompted speculation that the balance sheet reduction would be inconsistent with easier monetary policy. Second, the size of the reverse repo facility has recently dipped below \$300 billion for the first time since early 2021. This should imply that more of future reductions in the Fed’s assets will be met by declines in bank reserves, rather than in the repo facility.

In past communications Chair Powell has indicated that rate cuts would be consistent with ongoing reductions in the Fed’s balance sheet. By his reasoning both would be normalization of policy: the funds rate normalizing by moving closer to neutral, the balance sheet by moving closer to the minimal size consistent with ample bank reserves. This logic only holds as long as the funds rate is above neutral—a state we expect will be reached well into 2025.

We estimate that the producer price index (PPI) rose 0.2% in July. Energy prices fell notably in June but we expect a 1.3% rebound last month that would reverse about half of the decline. Food prices have generally eased in recent months, and we expect a 0.2% decline in July that would be similar to the drop in June. Away from food and energy, we forecast that the core PPI rose 0.2% last month with broadly similar contributions from both core goods and core services. Nonetheless, if realized this would constitute the softest reading in four months, and would help contribute to a slowing of yearago core PPI inflation to 2.6% from 3.0% in June. Headline PPI inflation is also expected to slow to 2.3%oya in July from 2.6% previously.

We expect that the consumer price index (CPI) rose 0.2% in July. This would be the firmest print in three months, reflecting some firmness in food and particularly energy prices that is due to sources other than gasoline for a change. The former likely rose 0.2% last month while the latter are expected to have increased 0.5% following two consecutive months of more sizable declines. Away from food and energy, we look for the core CPI to have risen 0.17% in July, which would be somewhat above the prior two months but still broadly consistent with a cooling in the underlying pace of inflation seen over the past few months. If realized, the year-ago core CPI inflation rate should moderate to 3.2% (from 3.3% in June) as a result. Headline CPI inflation is expected to remain at 3.0% on a year-ago basis in July.

Following the large step-down in the monthly growth rates of shelter-related CPI components in June, we look for a modest give-back in July that would nonetheless keep the recent downward trend intact. We forecast that tenants’ rent rose 0.30% last month while owners’ equivalent rent (OER) increased 0.32% in July. Also within shelter, prices for lodging away from home are expected to decline for the fourth straight month as travel and related demand gradually cools from its earlier very elevated pace. We look for some further moderation in medical care prices with a slight 0.1% rise on the month, and for the recently enacted jump in postage stamp prices to put measurable upward pressure on the communication CPI index.

The other main categories of the core CPI are expected to show falling prices on the month for July. Two sizable sources of downward pressure on the CPI over the past year or so are likely to once again post sizable declines in July: used vehicles and airfares. Used vehicles have been a persistent drag on the CPI, and we look for a -2.2% reading for last month—which would be the largest monthly decline since the start of this year. We look for a small decline in new vehicle prices as well. Airfares have come off sharply in recent months, and we expect another decent-sized drop in July to contribute to a 2.5% plunge in public transportation—the third straight month of large declines in this category. We also look for a 0.2% fall in apparel prices, which have been broadly moving sideways in recent months after a surge earlier this year.

Payroll survey week</td></tr></table>

We expect that initial jobless claims will be largely unchanged at 235k in the week ending August 10. Claims fell 17k last week, in part due to a normalization of unusually high claims in Texas and Michigan, with the claims in Texas likely still stemming from Hurricane Beryl and the ones in Michigan possibly linked to auto plant shutdowns. Claims in those two states could still decline a little further, but there may be an offset from claims related to Hurricane Debby. Debby made landfall in Florida as a Category 1 hurricane on August 5, though it was quickly downgraded to a tropical storm. It then made landfall again as a tropical storm in the Carolinas on August 8.

Continuing claims will be important to watch as after a number of weeks of stability they punched higher again in the week ending July 27, reaching a new peak for the year. The rise in continuing claims suggests that re-employment probabilities could be declining, which is also the message from labor market flows data in the Current Population Survey. Note that it is better to focus on continuing claims than the insured unemployment rate, which is continuing claims divided by covered employment. The source of covered employment statistics is the QCEW, which is only available through December, so the Labor Department has to use the trend in covered employment from 2023 to project the growth in covered employment in 2024 (with this being updated as new QCEW reports come out each quarter). Consequently, any slowdown in employment that may have occurred in 2024 will not be immediately captured, which could bias down the insured unemployment rate.

1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv

We project that retail sales rose 0.4%m/m in July, driven by a 2% gain in auto sales. The report should otherwise be moderately weak, with sales excluding autos and gas down 0.1%, though this would be after a strong 0.8%m/m increase the prior month. Informed by spending data on Chase cards, we look for sales in the control category (excludes food services, autos, gas, and building materials) to have increased 0.1%, and for food services sales to decline a sizable 0.7%m/m, which for that store category would be the worst reading since January. We also expect a small decline in sales at building material stores. Sales and employment at building stores had each broken a downward trend at various points in 1H24, but employment declined again in both June and July.

July retail sales will provide the first updated track on real consumer spending growth in 3Q. Our current 3Q forecast is for growth of 1.1%q/q, saar, but given a positive monthly trajectory through the second quarter, this could be achieved even if real spending were to simply stay flat to June, so there is some upside risk.

<table><tr><td rowspan="5">Thu Aug 15 8:30am Import prices %oya</td><td>Import prices %m/m, nsa, unless noted</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Apr 1.0</td><td>May -0.2</td><td>Jun 0.0</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>1.1</td><td>1.4</td><td>1.6</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td>0.2</td><td>1.6</td></tr><tr><td>Ex-fuel import prices</td><td>0.7</td><td>-0.3</td><td></td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>0.9</td><td>0.5</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.9</td></tr></table>

We forecast that the import price index was unchanged in July. This would mark the second straight month of no monthly change in the headline index, but the underlying details look notably different between June and July. Whereas imported energy prices fell on the month in June, we look for them to have risen in July. By contrast the nonfuel import price index is expected to have declined modestly in July, against a flat reading in June. As a result, we see the year-ago growth in the headline import price index remaining at 1.6%, while the annual growth rate for nonfuel import prices likely slid modestly to 0.9%. If realized, these outcomes would point to a potential peaking in the year-ago inflation rates for these import price indexes last month, which would be aligned with the lagged appreciation observed in the US trade-weighted dollar index on a year-ago basis.

We look for a 0.5%m/m decline in industrial production in July, with drags from auto production, mining, and utilities, coupled with a 0.1%m/m increase in manufacturing excluding motor vehicles. Motor vehicle production had been slowly increasing as automakers have rebuilt depressed inventories, but production schedules indicate a decline in output in July. Excluding motor vehicles, manufacturing had been slowly trending down since early 2022 as production of equipment, particularly industrial equipment, has declined. However, output has jumped in the last couple months, driven especially

by gains in nondurable goods manufacturing, including ongoing increases in chemicals, which include pharmaceuticals. Aggregate hours worked in the July payroll report for manufacturing excluding motor vehicles were down 0.1%, but we expect output will be a little stronger. It is possible that hours worked during the payroll week (the week including the 12th) were slightly depressed compared to the rest of the month by Hurricane Beryl.

Utility output probably fell in July given a seasonally adjusted decline in electricity output, along with temperatures that were less unusually warm than in June. Mining output should also decline given that hours worked in this sector fell, and crude oil production and active rig counts also dropped.

We expect that starts and permits both declined close to 3%m/ m in July. Overall starts and permits were pulled up in June by a rise in the multifamily segment, which was coming off low levels, and given the normal volatility in this series it could retreat some in July. Multifamily starts had steadily declined through early this year as builders downshifted from the rapid pace of building in 2022 and 2023, and starts are now back at more normal levels.

For single-family homes, builders may continue to reduce starts in the near-term given that inventories and months’ supply are continuing to rise, and the ratio of starts to sales is still higher than average. The NAHB housing market had also edged down last month, signalling that builders were seeing slightly lower sales volumes and prospective buyer traffic. Single-family starts already declined 19%q/q saar in 2Q.

We look for the Michigan sentiment index to have fallen to 65.0 in the preliminary August survey from 66.4 in July. There are two factors that may have been especially influential for sentiment in the August report, whose survey dates are from July 24 to August 14. The first is the withdrawal of President Biden from the 2024 presidential race, which has probably raised sentiment among Democratic-leaning respondents, which would argue for a rise in the index. However, the other factor is the large level of market volatility and negative headlines following the July employment report on August 2, which could have reduced sentiment.

Recall that the Michigan survey was moving from phone to web surveys over the last four months, a process that is now completed. The University of Michigan estimated that this could have lowered sentiment over that period by 6-7pts given that web respondents typically have more negative responses, especially on current conditions.
