The US nominal goods and services trade balance narrowed from -\$75.0bn in May to -\$73.1bn in June, driven by a narrowing in the goods trade balance that was largely known based on the already released advance trade report. Values in this report are close to what was assumed in the first 2Q GDP print, and we only see this report taking off -0.02% from annualized GDP growth. Previously we estimated that last week’s construction report added 0.04%, so on net 2Q GDP is still tracking close to its original 2.8%q/q saar value (it was 2.84% unrounded, so given the positive benefit from construction it may round up to 2.9%).

# Focus: Concerning signs as balance sheets normalize

Relatively healthy balance sheets and accumulated pandemicera liquid wealth provided support for consumer spending during the pandemic recovery. But as concerns about labor market weakness emerge, the recent health of household balance sheets is gaining increasing attention. The 2Q data this week from the Federal Reserve Bank of New York’s Consumer Credit Panel offer some new insights on household debt and credit usage. Even though total debt and delinquencies point to normalization so far, a further deterioration in credit card and auto loan delinquencies paints a cautiously concerning picture.

Aggregate household debt increased by \$109bn in 2Q, up by 4.3%oya according to the latest NY Fed report. Given the high mortgage rates, it was not surprising that mortgage debt increased by only \$77bn, up 4.2%oya. Credit card loans, which grew briskly until about a year ago, slowed down a bit, but home equity lines of credit (HELOC) surged over the last year, increasing by 11.8% (Figure 1). This was the strongest pace since 2006. It is possible that some homeowners with equity who have low existing mortgage rates utilized this credit availability instead of relying on cash-out refinancings as they could have done in the past. Overall, it appears that while some households started to rely on credit card debt to smooth consumption, some homeowners with enough equity chose to tap into that source.

Figure 1: Change in household debt balances
![](images/cff3733eb420f4cc10a97b73e04f8b71226b6254c84399855182456685448d1a.jpg)

Rather than utilization, it is the increasing delinquency rates for auto loans and credit cards in the report is that are somewhat concerning (Figure 2). Even though the overall delinquency rate looks to be largely normalizing to pre-pandemic levels, that could be partly due to historically low mortgage (not shown) and student loan delinquencies. The former could be attributed to the fact that a large number of households locked in low interest rates during the pandemic. Historically low rates for student loans, on the other hand, are due to administrative policies delaying the reporting of missed payments until 4Q24, and might be artificially low right now.

Figure 2: New delinquent (30+ days) balances by loan type % of current balances
![](images/8016bb6642ee53e36174dca5aca55b3dbc52e1ecbb80de7636609c51cafdddb6.jpg)

Measurement issues can be a challenge in determining the share of balances delinquent, especially with the data reported through credit bureaus. But lagged data (last point for 1Q24) from the Federal Reserve Board on all commercial banks also show a similar pattern of rising delinquencies for credit card loans, albeit not at the same pace or to the same elevated level (Figure 3). The patterns in Figure 3 for credit card delinquencies could reflect a given number of financially-constrained borrowers falling behind on increasingly larger credit balances, or it could be more borrowers becoming delinquent. Looking at the transition rate into credit card delinquency by age, it appears that transition rates across all age cohorts, but especially for prime-aged 30-59 year old individuals, have increased rather sharply over recent quarters (Figure 4).

Figure 3: Delinquency rate for credit cards
![](images/3ed3d0666ada17b946bacdbd79023fa891445a119fc9a1b44ef1b23f5064a620.jpg)

Figure 4: Credit card transitions to serious delinquency by age 4q moving sum
![](images/2934f7993ad2f194188b05d9730500ab3964e60a6c6619ea23649bebb52f6208.jpg)

## Euro Area

• Euro area data continue the theme of subdued growth

Fall in PMI was confirmed, retail sales trend up only slightly and German industry remains weak

ECB clearly set to cut rates, but we still expect only quarterly 25bp cuts for now

In contrast to the rise in US growth risks, the Euro area data continue to show more continuity. This week’s reports confirmed a second consecutive fall in the composite PMI to a level signalling just below 1% growth, showed only a modest upward trend in retail sales and revealed further disappointment in German industry. But, none of of this is decisive enough to change our forecast beyond the recent marking down of 3Q24 to the 1%ar we now expect for the next four quarters. The focus in the near term is on the flash PMI for August in two weeks. Our forecast requires the PMI to find a bottom and, ideally, improve slight from the July level. We think that fundamentals are supportive of this, but there is risk of a further deterioration, and the PMI will be an important data release.

Even if the PMI improves in August, it would still be below the ECB staff projection that has GDP growing at a 1.5%ar. If GDP growth is closer to a 1%ar, as in our forecast, it would clearly make the two cuts we have in our forecast for September and December very likely. But, is the recent market repricing and concern about the US economy shifting the ECB’s thinking towards signalling a third cut for October? There has been very little ECB commentary since the recent market repricing. Of note are the comments made by Bank of Finland Governor Rehn. He signalled some concern on growth, but still suggested that rate cuts depended on the disinflation process remaining on track, while describing recent market moves as an over-reaction. He is supportive of further cuts, but it is not clear that he now expects larger or faster cuts than before.

## Thoughts on possible Trump tariff impacts

While the outcome of the US election remains unclear, a Trump victory could spell another round of US import tariffs. Trump has threatened 60% tariffs on all imports from China and 10% tariffs on imports from all other countries, including the EU.

Tariffs can impact the Euro area through a number of channels. First, they are a tax on trade and that tax has to be paid by someone. The response to the tax can include reduced spending overall, shifts in the pattern of spending, shifts in the location of production, etc. Second, an uneven imposition of tariffs across countries can lead to diversion effects (e.g. if new tariffs are highest on US-China trade some third countries may benefit in those two markets). Third, tariffs can disrupt global production chains if tariffs are high or focused on individual products. Fourth, tariffs can lead to sentiment and uncertainty effects, and possibly to some financial stress. This is especially the case if there is a threat of further tariffs. Finally, by having an impact on growth and sentiment across countries and creating uncertainty, tariffs can trigger currency moves that can affect the overall economic outcomes.

Many of the effects above depend on the size of the tax, including via retaliatory moves, which depends on the size of the trade flows. Table 1 shows key magnitudes. A 10% tariff imposed by the US on €451bn of imports from the Euro area would be a tax of 0.3% of Euro area GDP, while a 10% retaliatory tariff by the EU would raise 0.2% of GDP in tax revenue on the €317bn of imports from the US.

## Table 1: Euro area exports

2023 values, memo: 2023 GDP was €14,428bn
<table><tr><td></td><td colspan="3">Exports</td><td colspan="3">Imports</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>€bn</td><td>% total</td><td>% GDP</td><td>€bn</td><td>% total</td><td>% GDP</td></tr><tr><td>National Accounts</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total exports</td><td>7588</td><td>100.0</td><td>52.6</td><td>7031</td><td>100.0</td><td>48.7</td></tr><tr><td>ow: Services</td><td>2316</td><td>30.5</td><td>16.1</td><td>4921</td><td>70.0</td><td>34.1</td></tr><tr><td>ow: Goods</td><td>5272</td><td>69.5</td><td>36.5</td><td>2110</td><td>30.0</td><td>14.6</td></tr><tr><td>Trade data (goods)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total exports</td><td>5503</td><td>100.0</td><td>38.1</td><td>5443</td><td>100.0</td><td>37.7</td></tr><tr><td>Intra</td><td>2848</td><td>51.7</td><td>19.7</td><td>2848</td><td>52.3</td><td>19.7</td></tr><tr><td>Extra</td><td>2655</td><td>48.3</td><td>18.4</td><td>2788</td><td>51.2</td><td>19.3</td></tr><tr><td>ow: US</td><td>451</td><td>8.2</td><td>3.1</td><td>317</td><td>5.8</td><td>2.2</td></tr><tr><td>ow: China</td><td>202</td><td>3.7</td><td>1.4</td><td>423</td><td>7.8</td><td>2.9</td></tr></table>

Source: Eurostat, J.P. Morgan

There are many possibilities in terms of who pays these tariffs. US and Euro area consumers would pay if the taxes are fully passed through. Alternatively, producers or importers/ exporters would pay if they absorb the tariffs in their margins to protect their market share. Currency moves can also affect the overall impact by generating broader relative price effects. If currency moves are small and the cost of the retaliatory tariffs falls fully on Euro area consumers, it would be a loss of purchasing power of only 0.3% of their disposable income. This would be relatively modest for growth and also for inflation, which could temporarily increase by 0.3%-pt.

In reality, tariffs would likely lead to some volume effects. If currency moves are small, Euro area producers would lose competitiveness due to the tariff against domestic producers in the US whilst gaining competitiveness against Chinese exporters to the US (due to the higher 60% tariff applied to them). At the same time, Euro area producers would gain competitiveness against US producers in the Euro area and Chinese markets. These effects depend on the price elasticity of the products and their substitutability. But, even the direct effects versus US producers in the US market would be capped by the modest size of the tariffs in terms of total Euro area exports and GDP.

In a recent media article, the EU has reportedly estimated that annual exports could decline by €150bn if the US imposes a 10% tariff. This would amount to around 5% of Euro area exports to outside the region. When tariffs on steel (25%) and aluminium (10%) were imposed by the US in June 2018, Euro area exports of these products significantly underperformed exports of other goods to the US and total exports to the world (Figure 1). Hence, the estimate of €150bn for the EU as a whole may be reasonable, but as the tariff would apply to all imports into the US, it is less clear if domestic producers could replace imports across all product categories, and Euro area producers would pick up some market share from US exporters in other markets and from China in the US. We therefore suspect that the effect would be smaller.

Figure 1: Euro area exports
![](images/75dd8a59963899151d1678e0ef926ae90b9049bfb6832fa92e683f1c933b85b2.jpg)

## Three important wild cards:

Currency moves may add pressure. While currency moves are hard to predict, it is plausible to assume that the euro would appreciate against CNY and depreciate against USD. The latter may not provide much of a growth offset as imported energy, which is often denominated in USD, would become more expensive, hurting Euro area manufacturers. We have tended to assume that a 10% trade-weighted currency appreciation reduces Euro area GDP by 1% relative to the baseline and that this effect takes around two years to be fully felt. However, the USD and CNY make up only a third of the Euro area broad trade-weighted exchange rate, which dampens the overall effect.

Sentiment could deteriorate significantly. A sense, that sentiment can amplify the impact of trade tensions is often informed by the experience of 2018/19. Euro area growth certainly slowed sharply from a very strong 3%ar in 2017 to just a 1%ar in 2018/19. In our PSR framework, global growth and sentiment explain almost 1%-pt of this slowing, but GDP also outperformed the PSR in 2017 and underperformed it in 2018/19 (Figure 2). This is also the case when tracking GDP with the PMI: a part of the slowing in actual GDP growth is hard to explain, reflecting surprising weakness in 2018 in particular. We also note that the economy was unlikely to sustain the very strong 2017 growth even without the trade war, while other factors may also have played a role (e.g. the risk of a hard Brexit, regulatory pressures in the car sector). This raises questions about the true sentiment effect of the trade war, which we would put, at most, at 0.5% of GDP.

Figure 2: Euro area real GDP growth and PSR model
![](images/d32033e8dc790401736034607e040ccb0ad64c72eae44ac8b4840efb43d924d1.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, J.P. Morgan

A broader retaliation against others is possible. In responding to any new US tariffs, the EU is likely to follow the 2018 playbook, which is to impose high tariffs (e.g. 50%) on goods that may hurt Trump politically, whilst keeping the total volume of tariffs below those imposed by the US. But, before retaliating, the EU may seek negotiations and offer to buy more US products, which it is already doing in LNG, and it could support the US in some areas (e.g. as it did in 2018 in the area of Chinese technology transfers). Beyond the US, the EU may also launch new investigations if it thinks that a CNY depreciation materially amplifies strategic challenges posed by Chinese competition, as has already been the case with EVs. But, we suspect that such retaliation would be relatively slow moving, while an EU-wide fiscal response is unlikely, especially in a pre-emptive way.

Overall, the impact of a broader trade war is still difficult to gauge. Direct effects are likely to be small as the scale of tariffs would be modest (10% into the US) and because Euro area producers would gain competitiveness against US and Chinese producers in some markets. At the same time, currency, sentiment and broader retaliation effects (e.g. against China due to a possible CNY depreciation) could amplify the total effect, but, these are more uncertain. Another relevant consideration is the state of the manufacturing sector. From a profit perspective, the sector has performed well, even in Germany, but, production has been weak for a while which may raise questions about the sector’s ability to absorb another shock.

While the “other transport equipment” category gained 11.7%m/m, the ex-bulk category was also solid with an increase of 3.3%m/m. The increases were broad-based although uneven in magnitude across products, with a

9.3%m/m jump in motor vehicle orders standing out on the upside. More positively, domestic orders were particularly firm in June. The main reason to be cautious is that the orders data are currently running well ahead of the much weaker new orders index of the German manufacturing PMI. At around 42pts in June and July, it is signalling a 5% annualised pace of decline of orders.

Total IP increased 1.4%m/m, in line with our forecast, but this reversed only half of a downwardly-revised fall of 3.1%m/m in May and left total IP with a decline of 5.1%q/q saar in 2Q24. This makes the disappointment in last week’s flash GDP reports (-0.3%q/q saar) less surprising.

The recent weakness in total IP includes a weather-related drag from construction (-10.4%q/q saar in 2Q24), which had been boosted by 10.5%q/q saar in 1Q24, but manufacturing was also down 4%q/q saar in 2Q24. The latter is despite a 1.4%m/m increase in June, which reflected an 8.7%m/m rebound in motor vehicle production and a further improvement in energy-intensive production. There were also gains in some other categories, but overall the “other” part of manufacturing slipped further. In addition, we already know from the VDA car production data that a further setback is likely in July, which could, however, continue the recent run of what looks like holiday-related ups and downs. Flooding in parts of Germany in late May and early June may have played a role as well as it has caused some disruptions at some auto suppliers.

<table><tr><td>Retail sales</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total sales, volumes</td><td></td><td></td><td>0.1</td><td></td><td>I</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.1</td><td></td><td>0.5</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya, working-day adj.</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.3</td><td></td><td></td><td>-0.3</td></tr></table>

Euro area retail sales declined 0.3%m/m in June, which left 2Q24 with an increase of 1.1%q/q saar. The recent trend is modestly upward, while consumer spending has grown a bit faster and from a lower level. This suggests more momentum in services spending than in goods spending, but it also suggests that consumer spending continues to disappoint relative to what are strong real income gains, the steady improvement in confidence and signs of some improvement in credit demand from the ECB bank lending survey.

An important caveat is that Eurostat does not currently have German data for May or June owing to a technical problem at the German statistics office which has delayed those reports (likely) until late in August. Of course, it is not clear how the inclusion of German data will affect the Euro area aggregate, but we note that they made a solid start to 2Q24 in April, which could reflect a pick-up in real income gains that has come quite late in Germany due to the structure of its pay negotiation schedule. In addition, part of June could benefit from the Euro 2024 football tournament, but Eurostat is currently likely to be assuming 0%m/m gains from Germany for both May and June due to a lack of data.

## Japan

BoJ baseline of gradual policy normalization challenged by external uncertainty

• Absent hard-landing recession scenario, we continue to see slow move toward 1% policy rate

Liquidity conditions to tighten on BoJ balance sheet rundown; watching for further spillovers into global markets

• Next week: 2Q GDP bounces back on consumption recovery

While we expected the BoJ to raise rates and begin its QT process last week, we did not anticipate its contribution in fueling a sharp and near-immediate reaction in markets, centered around rapid yen appreciation. The trade-weighted yen is up close to 10% from its trough around a month ago, largely reflecting the impact of a sharp unwind of yen-funded leveraged carry trades over the past several days. Against this backdrop, we think there are three points worth making on capital flows and liquidity; on growth and inflation; and on the BoJ’s reaction function.

First, in a baseline where the BoJ continues gradually to raise the policy rate and continues with balance sheet rundown, the cost of yen funding will rise and the flow of liquidity from Japan into global markets will decline. This dynamic will have important lingering implications for capital flows and, potentially, for asset prices. The QT process is now fully baked into the cake, as per the July policy meeting’s commitment to a sustained decline in bond purchases. Thus, despite recent market volatility, the recent fall in domestic yields suggests that the BoJ has room to continue to reduce bond buying as per its plan.

Figure 1: Japan M0 and BoP net foreign portfolio invest. assets JPY tn, chg. oya JPY tn, 12m sum, 12m lag
![](images/ec5bad5653c92b65769eb289cd9c0f21c67f837489309d466ae07dcb4e52ea51.jpg)

As the BoJ drains system liquidity for the first time in more than a decade by shrinking its balance sheet, falling excess liquidity and a subsequent declining Japanese bid for foreign assets (and possible repatriation of existing assets into domestic assets) risks combining with further unwind of yen-funded carry trades (Figure 1). This dynamic marks a potentially important inflection point; we laid out the mechanics in more detail in this note a few weeks back.

Second, while market moves have been extremely large over the past week, their influence on Japan’s growth and inflation trajectory will depend on whether volatility remains confined to markets, or whether we are slipping into a sharper slowing in US/global growth momentum. If the former, we think it important not exaggerate the impact of the past week’s moves on Japan’s real economy. We estimate a sensitivity of around 0.5%-pts on core CPI to each 10% move in the yen. Mechanistically, if USD/JPY stabilizes around current levels (and given lags in the pass-through from imported inflation), we see slight downside risk to our 1Q25 3.5%oya core (ex. fresh food) inflation forecast to the tune of around 0.8%-pts. But if growth holds up, this moderation in goods inflation may be more than offset by strong momentum in wage growth and thus services inflation. This week’s wage data was a case in point, with headline wage growth surging into June. Rising incomes are likely to have contributed to a solid consumption bounce in next week’s 2Q GDP report.

Third, we believe that, absent the hard-landing or recession scenario, the BoJ remains on a gradual path toward a neutral policy rate of at least 1%. In this context, Deputy Governor Uchida’s comments this week moved markets; he stated that the BoJ “will not raise its policy interest rate when financial and capital markets are unstable.” But his remarks were notable in what they thus implied: that the Bank could raise rates once the dust had settled. This was a message reinforced by the summary of opinions from the July policy meeting. And they are in line with our own view of a gradual, but still-moving, BoJ absent the US hard-landing/recession scenario. While external risks over our forecast horizon have clearly risen, we continue to see a baseline in which the policy rate increases by 25bp every 6 months or so, or every 3-4 meetings.

## BoJ board emphasizes path toward neutral

The summary of opinions from the July Policy Meeting confirmed that economic developments are generally in line with the Bank's outlook, supporting its decision to hike rates last week. Key members acknowledged the current stance of monetary policy as "highly accommodative." Opinions noted that “real interest rates are at their most negative levels in the past 25 years, and the degree of monetary accommodation, based on various indicators, has been significantly above its average during the period of QQE.” Other opinions described the policy rate as "significantly low" and indicated that “the nominal interest rate will continue to be at a highly accommodative level of 0.25%” even after the decision. This is not surprising to us. Despite higher inflation exceeding the BoJ's target for an extended period, the BoJ has made limited adjustments to the policy rate.

The BoJ's strategy involves gradually retreating from the high degree of easing if the economy is confirmed to be on a solid footing. One opinion suggested that “assuming that the price stability target will be achieved in the second half of fiscal 2025, the Bank should raise the policy interest rate to the level of the neutral interest rate toward that time. As the level of the neutral rate seems to be at least around 1%, in order to avoid rapid hikes in the policy interest rate, the Bank needs to raise the policy interest rate in a timely and gradual manner, while paying attention to how the economy and prices respond.” This suggests, under the BoJ’s strategy to address structural shifts in inflation and wages, “in line with the Bank's outlook” is the necessary condition for gradual rate hikes, but cyclical price and economic upside surprises are not necessarily required. This is also in line with our own forecast for gradual policy normalization.

The rate hike decision had two dissenting votes, but these were not against the hike itself, only the timing. The dissenting members wanted to confirm more economic data and preferred to decide at the next meeting. There thus appears to be a consensus among members on the long-term direction of monetary policy hikes.

Comments on upside risks to inflation were also notable. The risk of price increases owing to tightening supply and demand in some industries and a tight labor market was mentioned. An opinion listed at the top of the Monetary Policy section highlighted that “the year-on-year rate of change in import prices has turned positive again, and upside risks to prices require attention.” Post-meeting currency movements may have partially mitigated these risks, but the BoJ is increasingly aware that structural shifts could lead to higher inflation than previously expected.

## Wage growth surges as trend heads higher

Headline June wage data were much stronger than we and the consensus expected, with total earnings up 4.5%oya, against our forecast of a 1.8% rise, and consensus expectation of a 2.4% increase. This was the strongest headline number since June 1994. Most of the upside surprise reflected a rise in volatile special payments, but there was an encouraging further trend increase in scheduled pay growth. Constant sample data (which removing sampling volatility) also pointed to strong wage growth of 5.4%oya, more than double its May growth rate (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Japan Total cash earnings
![](images/804fe78c7fa5f67292088799b6f079e74f673d1e0d66f97650f031b183f000a8.jpg)

Scheduled earnings rose 2.3%oya, broadly in line with our expectations, led by a sustained trend rise in payments for full-timers, in line with the strong result of the Shunto wage negotiations. Full-timers scheduled pay grew 2.7%oya, its fastest pace since November 1992. Part-timers’ hourly earnings also rose a solid 4.9%. Special payments, which accounted for more than 40% of the rise in headline wages in June, surged 7.8%oya, reflecting historically high summertime bonuses (Figures 3 and 4).

Figure 3: Japan Composition of total cash earnings JPY thousands, both scales, sa
![](images/d1f907b41584984c230d95f3c927fe98e58004890a01125bd9e787a9125e7165.jpg)

Figure 4: Japan Scheduled earnings composition %oya, both scales
![](images/bdf18ee088f8854d79110edb6edc70039fd25fb6c3a9268816317a5f056271c2.jpg)

With the upside surprise in total wages, real wages turned positive for the first time since April 2022, rising 1.1%oya. While this in part reflected transitory factors, we continue to expect base pay to rise toward a 3%oya pace, consistent with the strong result of the Shunto wage negotiations and sustained labor market tightness. Also, the government’s 5% proposal for an increase in minimum wages from October will further push up part-timers’ earnings. If special payments remain strong next month, high bonus payments could again push up total wage growth in July.

Given volatility in the headline wage data, real wages may flip between positive and negative growth rates over the next few months. But on a trend basis, continued rises in nominal base wages should leave real wage growth positive toward the end of the year on a more sustained basis.

## Consumption recovery underway

We are seeing more concrete signs of a consumption recovery in line with our judgment that households are increasing spending through midyear. Consumer sentiment has improved since June, reflecting higher wages and the government’s one-off tax cuts. This week, the BoJ’s Consumption Activity Index (CAI, travel balance adjusted) also rose 0.8%m/m in June, with some revisions in the data for earlier months (April: 1.0 to 1.1%, May: 0% to -0.5%).

These revisions leave the 2Q average 3.1%q/q, saar above 1Q, posing modest upside risks to our 2Q GDP-based consumption forecast of 2.0%. Looking at the details, the durable goods index marked its third consecutive monthly rise, with a 3.4% rise in June, reflecting the easing of supply bottlenecks in autos that had dragged down consumption in 1Q. The nondurable goods and services indices also posted positive gains in June, rising 0.6%m/m for both (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Japan Real consumption indicators %q/q, saar, dashed line show JPM forecast
![](images/d7529c0d1a4e6e298ce23b2a5f13fc47a868bce4573f0e5e62da2fb7fa21608f.jpg)

In the July Economy Watchers survey, sentiment among small and medium enterprises (SMEs) rose 0.5pt to 47.5, following a rise in June. Though the level is still much lower than the beginning of the year, we see some recovery in sentiment after a deterioration in 1H. Details suggest that the businessrelated sentiment index led the improvement this month, rising 1.4pt to 48.7. The commentary suggests that this improvement is partly due to the rebound of a temporary production halt in the auto sector. The household-related index also rose 0.2pt to 47.2 following a large 2.2pt rise in June. We have

seen some positive signs in household consumption supported by higher wages, alongside a recovery in sentiment. The outlook index is also sending a positive signal ahead, rising 0.4pt to 48.3. Although SMEs are still concerned about rising input costs as well as higher wage costs, as suggested in the commentary, we expect to continue seeing a recovery in their business activities alongside a rise in domestic demand.

Source: ADA, JCSA, JDSA, JFA, JLM, VMA, Markit, METI, MHLW, MILT,MoF, Reuters, Statistics Bureau, J.P. Morgan forecasts

## Japan Focus: Sectoral divergence in wages

In the June employers survey, part-timers’ scheduled hourly earnings surged by 4.9%oya, marking the strongest increase in the cycle (Figure 1). At the same time, average hours worked have been declining. This may suggest that employers are reducing workers’ hours to manage their total wage bill.That some workers are receiving higher pay but are working fewer hours suggests their total pay packet may not be increasing overall. This may be one reason for what has been a weak run in consumption.

We see some divergence across sectors. Typically low-wage sectors including restaurants and retail have seen the largest recent wage gains (Figure 2), driven by a solid minimum wage hike. However, these industries have also seen the largest reduction in per-employee working hours (Figures 3 and 4). On the flip side, working hours have increased in the manufacturing and transport sectors, as these industries are highly dependent on human resources (Figure 5). Indeed, recent policy restrictions on overtime work in the transport sector have also increased pressure on part-timers’ working hours. Working hours in higher-margin industries (e.g., medical/health care services) appear less sensitive to higher wages (Figure 6).

## Canada

• July employment weakened more than expected for the second straight month...

• …but wage growth firmed and remains uncomfortably strong

Trade data suggest net exports will be a smaller drag on real GDP growth in 2Q24 than previously assumed

Today’s July Labour Force Survey indicated that the Canadian labor market continued to lose momentum at the start of 3Q24, and a mixed set of underlying details have likely made the Bank of Canada’s upcoming decisions more difficult. Job growth slowed more than expected and the unemployment rate held at a two-and-a-half year high of 6.4%. Yet sequential wage growth continued to firm, with the annualized three-month run rate of average hourly earnings for permanent employees accelerating to 7.0% from 5.8% in June. The 6.4% unemployment rate and disappointingly weak employment data—employment fell by 2,800 against expectations for a solid rise—alongside the stronger wage growth figures present conflicting signals for the Bank of Canada.

Despite the recent upward surprises to the run rates of both July wages and the June CPI (Figure 1), the BoC seems comfortable looking past these inflationary signals, retaining faith that a cooling labor market will ultimately pull down both of them. There is one more inflation report before the Governing Council convenes next month, but this was the final labor market update ahead of that policy meeting. We continue to see the BoC proceeding with a 25bp-per-meeting easing cycle at least through 1Q25, with the policy rate reaching 3% by mid-2025. That said, with softer job growth and expectations for a more dovish Fed, risks for a more aggressive easing pace by the BoC are rising—particularly if incoming economic data weaken more than expected.

Figure 1: Core services CPI and average hourly earnings %3m, saar; sa by J.P. Morgan
![](images/1d8af32d7278c85ee50f1f48ed31d9f7be8310f541b18f70a7343f22a078f18c.jpg)

The details of this week’s jobs report were quite volatile even by Canadian standards: part-time employment plunged 64.4K yet full-time work surged 61.6K in July. The participation rate also slipped a further 0.3%-pt to 65.0% while the employment rate (employees as a share of the over-15 population) fell for the ninth time in 10 months. The decline in July employment was concentrated in the services sector (-14.8K) with a partial offset from the goods sector (+12.1K). July declines in the services sector categories of wholesale and retail trade and accommodation and food services were partially offset by gains in public administration, information and recreation, and transportation and warehousing jobs. A modest decline in manufacturing employment was offset by broad-based job gains elsewhere in goods sector last month.

## A better balance for trade supports 2Q GDP

This week’s June international merchandise trade report suggested that trade was likely a smaller drag on growth last quarter than previously anticipated. Nominal exports jumped 5.5% in June while imports rose 1.9% on the month. As a result, the nominal merchandise trade balance unexpectedly moved from a C\$1.6bn deficit in May to a C\$0.6bn surplus in June. According to Statistics Canada, a 13.3% jump in exports of crude oil and a 35.3% surge in exports of gold accounted for more than three-quarters of the overall growth in total exports in June. A 5.1% drop in exports of motor vehicles and parts provided a partial offset to the overall increase. Meanwhile, imports of both energy products and motor vehicles and parts contributed to the rise in total imports in June, expanding 5.2% and 5.1%, respectively.

Figure 2: Real merchandise exports and imports %3m/3m, saar; chained 2017 C\$
![](images/c49115e824db4c29d760d685bf494a8d7cb16a3def0670d42a707c178c07b779.jpg)

According to our estimates, total exports in volume terms climbed 3.8% in June while total imports grew 1.3% (Figure 2). The rebound in real merchandise exports more than reversed May’s smaller 2.3% decline, but still left 2Q as a whole down 1.8%ar. Real merchandise imports rose 1.5%ar in 2Q. On the services side of the nominal trade balance, exports rose 0.6% and imports grew 1.6% in June. While not reported by Statistics Canada, real services exports were nearly flat while imports fell roughly 6%ar last quarter by our estimates. With the full set of monthly trade figures for 2Q24 in hand, net exports are now tracking a smaller drag on real GDP growth last quarter than previously anticipated.

## Mexico

• Banxico cut its reference rate by 25bp as we expected

• Core disinflation gains and soft growth empowered the board

• Inflation delivered mixed messages in July, mostly on non-core hiccups

Softer growth in the US to dent Mexico’s more than initially envisaged

This week, Banxico’s COPOM cut its policy rate by 25bp to 10.75% in a divided 3-2 decision—as we expected. The neutral (with a dash of dovishness) statement delivered on the forward guidance released in June, and emphasized the need to gradually ease the tight policy stance in the context of welcome news on the core inflation front.

With the real ex-ante rate still at 7% and monetary conditions only easing after the MXN correction triggered in June (Figure 1), the “too tight” argument remains compelling, in our view.

While the board did underscore the recent inflation concerns related to scattered spikes in raw food and energy, and the bouts of market volatility, the decision to cut confirms that the Board is focused on benign core inflation news, and its assumptions that ongoing shocks are temporary and that ongoing MXN corrections are not particularly concerning— even with the peso above 19.00. Also, with economic activity still delivering negative news for 2Q (and in the context of softer data in the US) the Bank had space to also emphasize downside risks to growth as the other key statement in the communique.

Figure 1: Monetary conditions index % change vs past five-year avg
![](images/f682e3ca47544bd75bbea8efb3115a222a0158aeec48abc1f2e0ca87f5350350.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from Banxico and Bloomberg Finance L.P.

The board underscored that the current inflation environment will likely allow it to discuss further changes to the policy rate, which reinforces our call for further cuts and confirms that the Board is chiefly focused on core dynamics and their gradual improvement (Figure 2) rather than headline, which the board revised up on the recent non-core shocks.

Going forward, the focus will be on the risk of some stickiness in core services (see below)and how MXN evolves in the ongoing global context (which will jeopardize core goods good behavior); however, with expectations that the FOMC will cut the policy rate from September onwards, we are comfortable with rate cuts in Mexico gaining traction gradually, except in November. We expect rates at 10.25% this year, and at 7.5% by the end of 2025.

Figure 2: Quarterly Core inflation path
![](images/4cc82befdabb771c33a50575df56d45f05afa5ded42c8c2060c991a6d5aec6d6.jpg)
Source: INEGI, Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Last update for Banxico Aug 8, 2024

## July inflation delivers mixed news

The July CPI print was going to be under special scrutiny ahead of this week’s Banxico policy meeting. But most of the attention had focused on whether there would be any relief in headline inflation from the recently raw food-induced spike and to what extent. By contrast, there was a sort of broad consensus that core inflation should stay the course. On the surface, we could say, both delivered; even if both headline and core came in a touch above expectations, the former eased a tenth to 5.5%oya and the latter dropped also about a tenth to 4.05%oya in the month.

However, beneath the surface data were challenging in two ways. First, core inflation picked up in the second half of the month (from 4.02% to 4.08%). Second, sequential data also posted a modest acceleration despite a continued, benign trend in core goods prices. By contrast, services price increases, which had moderated significantly, spiked again in July, with non-tourism prices leading the way up, rising more than 0.5%m/m, sa (Figure 3).

Back to the details, fruit and vegetable prices dropped in the second half of the month as we expected, though this was offset by meat and egg prices rising. High frequency data, however, suggest the decline in raw food extended into August, and hence this source of headline inflation pressure should be sidelined in the next couple of prints at least.

But core remains the focus. Core goods remain well behaved, and with weakness in global manufacturing still seemingly lingering we see no strong arguments to expect a dramatic

Industrial production

change of trend, even if some normalization is expected, more so amid FX normalization. The main issue lies within services inflation. The recent pickup is certainly concerning, but to the extent that we see domestic demand shifting gears lower in the second half of the year, we remain of the view that notwithstanding some bumps along the way, services sequential inflation should converge toward a 4% annualized rate by year-end.

Figure 3: Non-tourism services inflation
![](images/d82806ea65a8c7b7ec931eec271abe0ddbe12e0c83cacf998eef7eeeeb366482.jpg)

## Revising growth further down

Economic activity has disappointed since the end of last year, and early signs of pickup in the middle of last quarter were followed by weakness in June. The overall balance left GDP up merely 0.9%ar in 2Q, below our already tempered expectations for a 2% gain. The concerning part is this implied a 0.1% drop in the June GDP, setting activity on a weak trend into the current quarter. What’s more, contrary to prior quarters, when GDP weakness was entirely attributable to a large drag from net exports amid robust domestic demand, domestic demand slowed markedly in 2Q, specifically on the back of a seeming contraction in private consumption. This week’s IP report for June was a bit encouraging at 0.4%samr (2%saar) and suggests the 2Q final release at the end of the month could be marginally revised up.

Our baseline view against this pre-existing setup assumed a weaker path for domestic demand in 2H than we looked for before, but that was partly offset by a much smaller drag from net exports, as US import demand continued to shift from services to goods. The balance of risks around both legs of this call have now deteriorated on the back of downside risks to the US economy, and, particularly, the US labor market. The pass-through to private consumption should be nuanced but should be felt through lower remittances and tourism inflows (the former had already been weakening).

The more notable impact should be on net exports, as data suggest that global manufacturing, including that in the US, fails to properly recover and risks remain skewed toward prolonged weakness. The auto sector in particular had been among those showing more signs of pickup in past quarters, and should labor market weakness ensue, durable goods con sumption would likely be shed first, hitting local production.

These one-sided risks, combined with pre-existing ones of a political nature (the institutional reform agenda of the ruling party locally and the bilateral risks associated with the forthcoming USMCA review and immigration) that could weigh on investment, lead us to mark down our 2H average quarterly growth forecast to 0.75%ar from 1.25%, dragging full-year 2024 and 2025 growth projections down two-tenths each to 1.3%y/y and 1.0%, respectively (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Annual and quarterly GDP outlook
![](images/3ad6de7163eaa07370e1ce4497330130a4949ba97aaee7b0cab3444656ab235a.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan forecasts. \*Dotted line: annual average (%oya labeled); bars: observed seasonally adjusted annual rates (%q/q, saar)

## Brazil

• Outdated but hawkish COPOM minutes set the tone

• But global outlook justifies reassessing our rate path

• Assuming lower fiscal stimulus and growth, we now anticipate a moderate easing cycle next year

• After higher IPCA and change to our EOP forecast, watch out for June activity

Amid rising concerns about the state of global economy, all eyes turned to the minutes of COPOM’s July 31 meeting. Its tone was hawkish, with the board outlining a strategy whereby it will either stay put or hike, contingent not directly on relative monetary stance versus Federal Reserve or the BRL, but on its own inflation forecasts and balance of risks for the relevant horizon (six quarters ahead).

The vigilant and cautious message is an important one when BRL continues to stand out as one of the worst-performing currencies of the year—despite well-behaved terms of trade and solid fundamentals when we consider growth or the balance of payments (Figure 1). While it is true that fiscal imbal ances inspire caution, it is notable that the currency continues to hover closer to its most depreciated level for the year after the announcement of fiscal measures and an important, even if still insufficient, spending freeze.

Figure 1: REER and Terms of Trade Index, 2010=100, nsa, both scales
![](images/d23da3a186b904d17a9c79235abda8740bfe9b31366ae976d60f24dacf7197f7.jpg)

One of the highlights of the minutes related to the board members’ take on the balance of risks to the inflation forecast over the relevant horizon. While all admit that there are more risks to the upside for BCB’s inflation forecasts than to the downside (3x2), only some of them would go as far as to characterize such balance as asymmetrical to the upside. Therefore this is a not a unanimous take, and it is not even clear if it is the majority or not—a distinction that can influence September’s decision, which will be announced mere hours after the next Federal Reserve meeting.

One of the first board members to speak in public after the meeting, Deputy Governor Gabriel Galípolo explicitly mentioned that he sides with the more conservative members of the board, seeing the risks as asymmetrical. He also stressed that the board is totally data dependent and will do what is necessary to deliver a convergence (‘Galípolo procura eliminar dúvidas sobre como será eventual BC sob seu comando, Valor Econômico, August 08).

Contrasting the messages laid out by the board with our own forecasts, we remain of the view that rates will be kept at 10.50% per year over coming meetings, as a hike would require further deterioration of the inflation outlook (expectations and market prices)—a high bar in the absence of new shocks. But, following an updated house view on more aggressive Fed easing and its repercussions for economies across EM, we decided to reassess our monetary policy path—and not in the hawkish direction the minutes suggested.

While increasing our conviction in our call for stable rates in the near term, recent developments also opened room for discussing the relative stance and an easing cycle over the medium-term (Figure 2). We now see a window of opportunity for moderate cuts by mid-2025—in sharp contrast with the market pricing of over 120bp in hikes over the next 12 months.

Figure 2: SELIC rate path for 2024-2025
![](images/2494035f64f6036e6cf79cb9213330fcd2fbb6a487336dfd389ee14fc504b359.jpg)

A shift to easing will require not only an improvement of global financial conditions, but also of risk-sentiment regarding the Brazilian economy. And here we have the first important caveat. Easier financial conditions open room for lower rates, but they also reduce the incentives for more prudent fiscal policy in the medium term. Since this is the perpetual Achilles’ heel of the Brazilian economy, it will be important to monitor if the government will walk the talk over its degree of commitment to the fiscal policy framework over the several opportunities that it has between now and then.

The challenge is not small given the gap between the official targets, private-sector forecasts, and its consequences for the public debt trajectory over the medium term (Figure 3). The near-term signposts include, chiefly, the August 31, 2025,

budget, the announcement of measures to control the growth of mandatory expenditures, the bimonthly fiscal reviews, and the budget directives for 2026, a general election year, by April 2025.

Figure 3: Gross public debt
![](images/2fd32d7d73e2e2380d5931a4a02f0730053481fa7b8568cbc8206091acb0ced4.jpg)
Source: Planning Ministry, Central Bank and J.P. Morgan

In our view the window of opportunity for BCB’s board to ease will open up therefore not at the start of 2025, but by mid-year—when the combination of a restrictive policy stance and the expected fiscal drag should be accompanied by some deterioration in economic activity as well. Thus we are now working with a 100bp easing starting in June, taking the policy rate to 9.5% by the end of 2025. Importantly, we still see the need for a policy rate at restrictive levels by the end of 2025, as fiscal uncertainties are expected to remain throughout 2026 budget process, and activity is expected to only gradually slow down. The path is not straightforward, but the required degree of restrictiveness should be reduced amid what we expect to be easier global financial conditions.

## Higher core points to 4.2% year-end CPI

Following July’s more pressured CPI preview, the IPCA monthly gauge grew 0.38%—above our call and 3bp above consensus. As a result, headline IPCA accelerated 27bp from 4.2% to 4.5%, the same level seen at the start of the year (Figure 4). The core metric, while lower than in 1Q, accelerated for the second consecutive month and reached 3.8%oya. According to our m/m seasonally adjusted series, both core services and core goods started 3Q growing above the inflation target’s upper limit. So, despite a slowdown in diffusion, it is not easy to find reasons to take this week’s release as a good omen for 2H24 inflation.

Taking into consideration these results, we are changing our short-term IPCA forecasts. While anticipating a lower monthly result in August, in accordance with our high frequency tracking and recent announcements on energy tariffs (flags), we now assume higher prices for the remainder of the year. With that, our year-end projection now stands at 4.2%m 20bp above the previous 4%oya.

Figure 4: Headline and core IPCA forecasts
![](images/cccaa5005b11da89b134073d4dc8aae9f5f327e5a7a5f7c5d9d18c8141f6377e.jpg)

## Argentina

The cash-basis current account posted -US\$0.2bn in June, the first month in the red since October 2023

On a seasonally adjusted basis, lower exports explained the deterioration

• Commercial debt in 2Q24 would have been reduced to about US\$2.4bn

• Sequential tax collection bounced back in July, drive by foreign-trade revenues

• A (sustainable) rebound in economic activity becomes a necessary condition to support tax collection ahead

## Inflation to resume downward trend

We estimate July CPI at 4.0%m/m, driving the over-year-ago CPI print to 263%oya (Figure 1). Our forecast for headline inflation (INDEC’s National CPI) is a bit below the 4.1%m/m suggested by the high-frequency data set tracked by EcoGo through July-end. The monthly inflation print would thus resume the deceleration path after the slight rebound reported the prior month, on the back of regulated prices adjustments. Worth noting, City of BA CPI printed at 5.1%m/m in July, accelerating from the 4.8% reported the prior month. The difference with respect to our National CPI forecast is mainly explained by seasonal prices, which usually show a more variation in the City of BA than in the rest of the country due to July winter vacations (note a similar deviation was observed in July last year).

Figure 1: Headline CPI
![](images/24e8eebe0e3dd657df642bff714f967d2cd5c3ffc236da7ff1b83665865d59ee.jpg)

We expect core CPI to remain above the 3% monthly level. High-frequency data suggest core inflation will print at 3.7%m/m in July, flat from the prior month but below the 6.5% 3mma average through May. Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) should print at 4.2%m/m, relatively stable from 4.1% the prior month and below 6.6% 3mma through May.

Food inflation should see a modest rebound, accelerating to 3.6%m/m (1.1%-pt contribution), from the low 3.0% print reported last month. Adding granularity, the acceleration is likely mainly explained by vegetables (+9.0%m/m) on weather conditions, followed by beverages (+5.7%m/m) and meat prices (+4.1%m/m). The weekly prints have averaged 1%, sticky from 0.8% the prior month.

Regulated prices: up and down. After increasing in June, regulated prices were down again in July, printing at 6%m/m (a 1%-pt contribution). The monthly increase was still impacted by the increase in pre-paid medical services, cigarettes, water and communications. Yet, the scheduled increases of electricity and gas for July, as well as the increase in fuels tax were postponed, easing pressure on the headline print. Meanwhile, seasonal prices printed at 3.2%m/m, with the contribution relatively stable from the prior month (0.4%-pt) as the increase in vegetable prices was compensated by the decline in fruit prices (-2.6%m/m).

New regulated price adjustments were confirmed for August. The government announced electricity and gas tariffs will increase by around 4% on average in August, which adds to the adjustment higher in the price of fuels, water, and bus fares in AMBA scheduled for the month. In our estimates, the announced corrections jointly contribute 0.9%-pt to headline CPI. Against this backdrop, we see headline inflation proving sticky around a % monthly level in August.

## Tax collection gained in July

On a sequential basis, July’s real tax collection (J.P. Morgan’s seasonal adjustment) gained on the month, up by 3.5%m/m, sa, compared to a 3.9% monthly average gain in 2Q24. Thus, the 3-month sequential momentum is running at 68%3m/3m, saar, on base effects. Of note, the total tax revenues’ last 3m average level (sa) has increased 16% since the lows in February, though remains 4.2% below the levels observed in the 2016-2017 period (-12% when excluding the “PAIS tax” and foreign trade).

On an over-year-ago basis, real tax collection was down by 8.1%oya in July (when deflating by last 12-m inflation), making it a 6.9% contraction YTD. When we exclude foreigntrade related taxes and “PAIS” tax collection, total tax revenues are down by 13.3%oya YTD in real terms.

In the breakdown by revenue type, the PAIS tax has increased by 264.5%oya YTD, representing 6.1% of total tax collection throughout the year, from just 1.6% in same period last year. Yet, on a sequential basis, the PAIS tax revenues plunged in July when adjusting for seasonality, down by 39%m/m, sa, after the growth observed in the prior two months related to BOPREAL issuance. Meanwhile, foreign-trade tax collection increased by 19.9%oya in real terms YTD, spearheaded by export withholding taxes. On a sequential basis, foreign-trade tax collection saw a strong rebound on the month (+28%m/m, sa), on both higher exports and imports taxes. Last, consumption-related VAT revenues saw a modest reduction on the month after the strong rebound last month, with momentum stable at +11%3m/3m, saar.

As we highlighted in our recent note, the approval of the Omnibus bill and fiscal package in Congress prompted us to revise higher our 2024 annual primary surplus forecast to 1.2% of GDP on 0.7%-pt of GDP in additional fiscal revenues this year (to become effective since August). We now watch for the upcoming decision related to the PAIS tax, as the Ministry of Finance anticipated they could reduce it from 17.5% to 7.5% in September. If implemented from September, that reduction could result in 0.3%-pt of GDP in tax revenue sacrifice in our estimates.

Starting to think about next year, a (sustainable) rebound in economic activity becomes a necessary condition to support tax collection ahead. Indeed, when excluding foreign traderelated taxes, the PAIS tax, and also the one-off increase of the income tax in May due to the FX devaluation, the real contraction in tax revenues stands at 20.7%oya YTD (Figure 2). We estimate that the elimination of the PAIS tax—which legally expires in December—could imply around 1.3% of GDP less in revenues next year, while a more appreciated REER in 2025 (when compared to 2024) could imply a drag of around 0.3% of GDP in exports withholding taxes, all else equal. Finally, the one-off increase of the income tax on the back of year-end 2023 FX devaluation, of around 1%-pt of GDP, is likely not to be repeated next year. In all, we estimate a drag of around 2.5% of GDP in tax revenues for next year, which should be compensated in part by the fiscal package approved in Congress, but mostly by an economic activity rebound boosting tax revenues.

Figure 2: Total tax revenues
![](images/bfdf63253cd69ce6568e9d982dd5c1c6ccd4bc3c8e43c335f232c2a69fd94a4c.jpg)
Source: Ministry of Economy, AFIP and J.P.Morgan

The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Chile: Electricity prices drove the July CPI surge, and should continue to exert upward pressure ahead

• In terms of forecasts, the 2024 year-end headline CPI is projected at 4.5%oya

The CBC is expected to cut by 25bp in December; terminal rate is now projected at 4.5% by mid-2025

• Colombia: Jul ’24 CPI logged 0.20%m/m, and annual CPI resumed its downward trend

• We maintain our call for the policy rate to close the year at 8.50% (75bp cuts per meeting)

• We now expect the policy rate to converge to 6.5% by June ’25 and to 5.75% by Dec ’25

## Chile: Inflation, the Fed, and the CBC

Headline CPI logged 0.74%m/m in July (JPMe 0.65%, BBG consensus 0.6%), driving the last 12-m inflation (referential CBC splice) to 4.4%oya, from 3.8% in June. As expected, the month was marked by higher electricity prices, which added 27.6bp to headline CPI on the month. Moving to underlying inflation, ex. volatile CPI recorded 0.5% on the month, with the annual rate accelerating to 3.5%oya, from 3.4% by May. When adjusted for seasonality, the last 3-m annualized pace converged to 4.0%ar (see note for more details).

The adjustment in electricity prices has exerted less upside pressure on core than headline inflation, as we had expected. Therefore, while headline CPI is the policy target, focus on core will likely be increased in the coming months to assess the probability of headline convergence to target by the policy horizon. Thus, breaking down core inflation in goods and services gains more relevance than in prior months. It’s worth noting that core services have continued to decelerate, converging below pre-social unrest averages.

We fine-tune our 2024 year-end headline CPI forecast just 5bp higher, to 4.5%oya. We had estimated about 120bp in contribution from electricity prices through the end of next year, acknowledging the prevailing uncertainty also with regard to the timing of the adjustments within the next 12 months. We project headline inflation at 3.8%oya by Dec’25.

When it comes to monetary policy, the July print aligned with our base case. But the recent developments in the US labor market prompted changes in the risk distribution of inflation and growth ahead, thus meriting a quicker convergence to neutral fed fund rate levels (see note). Given the revisions, and absent a US recession in this second half of the year (to which we attach a 35% probability), we believe the CBC focus will be on headline and core inflation the months to

come. We thus maintain as base case the CBC resuming the easing cycle in December, with a 25bp cut. The change to our prior monetary policy path is that we now project a lower terminal rate at 4.5% by mid 2025, under the assumption of no US recession (cumulative probability 45% by 2H25).

## Colombia: BanRep—Inflation and the Fed

The Jul’24 monthly CPI printed 0.20%m/m, below our forecast and BBG consensus. Therefore, in line with our base case scenario, annual inflation resumed a downward trend. Indeed, after stalling around 7.2%oya in 2Q, the last 12-m print decelerated to 6.9%oya. When adjusting the time series for seasonality, headline CPI’s 3-month annualized pace decelerated to 6.5%, still above the target range ceiling. Regarding underlying inflation, core CPI as tracked by Ban-Rep (ex-food & regulated) gained 0.2%m/m, and on a 12- month basis decelerated to 5.8%oya (see note for more details).

Of relevance for our monetary policy call, core services continue to ease in gradually. Indeed, the sequential momentum for core services decelerated again, though to a still high 6.5%ar. Meanwhile, core goods sequential momentum ran at 1.7% ar and is closer to its pre-pandemic average. Ahead, we expect services inflation to continue its deceleration, though to levels well above pre-pandemic averages of around 4%ar.

We maintain our forecast of headline inflation converging to 5.4%oya by year-end, below the updated BanRep central scenario (5.7%oya). For next year, we foresee headline inflation converging to 3.8%oya by year-end (less optimistic than the 3.0% in BanRep’s central scenario). A key consideration is that we expect the bulk of diesel price adjustments to come next year, though acknowledge the prevailing uncertainty with regard to their magnitude and timing.

While we believe the primary focus for BanRep will continue to be the behavior of domestic prices, lower FFR is expected to provide more room to ease monetary conditions without risking a persistent acceleration of tradable inflation (via the interest rate differential). Given this context, we are maintaining the current policy rate path until the end of the year, with an acceleration in monetary easing in the upcoming meetings. We expect 75bp cuts per meeting in the last three meetings of the year, for the policy rate to converge to 8.5% by December. However, we have revised our projections for lower levels through 2025, again under the assumption of no US recession. We now expect the policy rate to converge to 6.5% by June 2025 (previously 7.0%) and to 5.75% by December 2025 (previously 6.25%).

Importantly, in addition to the prevailing uncertainty regarding the global economy, we stress two idiosyncratic risk fac-

Week of August 12 - 16

Peru

tors early next year. First are potential changes in the BanRep Board coming in February and the associated market perceptions regarding potential changes in its reaction function. A more heterodox reaction function in the face of prevailing fiscal risks (see note) could exacerbate downside pressures on the local currency, counteracting the desired effect of lower rates. Second is the risk of heightened uncertainty if the government moves ahead with the call to a Constituent Assembly, which could lead to a scenario of unrest similar to what Chile endured in 2020-22.

The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

Riots could affect some soft indicators, but 2011 experience didn’t leave a large data imprint

• REC and RICS survey remain soft but show some progress to recovery

• We leave our BoE forecast unchanged despite larger expected Fed easing

Domestic data warrant caution by the BoE, and US influence is not yet clear cut

The riots that have broken out over the past week pose some downside risk to near-term growth, but fundamentally the growth picture still looks favourable. The PMI advanced in July, the REC jobs report suggests employment is stagnant rather than falling, and expected sales in the RICS housing survey reached a new post-pandemic high. While downside growth risks in the US dominate discussions, there is little in the UK data to justify a faster pace of easing from the BoE. While changes to our Fed call are motivated by risk management, the domestic risks appear different in the UK and the direct impact of faster Fed easing in and of itself is likely limited in the UK. The broader question is whether US growth weakens materially and whether there is a common macro theme across the DM.

Next week's data will provide an update on the UK macro data. We look for non-bonus earnings growth to slows to 5.4%oya but remain firm on a m/m sequential basis. Headline inflation is likely to tick higher with core inflation falling to 3.3%. The BoE has recently deemphasized some of the key near term data points in thinking about the rate path, as it does intend to ease gradually in response to broader perceptions of moderating inflation risk. But we do not expect next week's data to alter risks materially.

## Looking at the 2011 data for riot impact

Violent street riots occurred 6th-11th August of 2011, including looting and arson. The numbers involved appear smaller this time. Based on the 2011 experience, we should probably expect some impact on the data this time round, although the magnitude is likely to be small and the range of affected indicators could be fairly narrow. For example, while the PMI did take a step down in August 2011 there was no discernible impact on overall GDP growth for the quarter (Figure 1). After falling 3.5-pts in August, the PMI only recouped 1.7-pts the following month, i.e. suggesting there were other more long lasting factors at play. Surprisingly, there wasn't a significant impact on consumer confidence, with the Gfk falling just one point in August 2011 and then recouping this the following month. Retail sales rose 0.3%m/m, rebounding from the prior month's weakness (Figure 2).

Figure 1: Effect of 2011 London Riots on PMI and GDP
![](images/9cb68263947d4af0d33a577efeabfc2a702fd1bb7dca89a0254c1188fadb08da.jpg)

Figure 2: Gfk consumer confidence and retail sales
![](images/9b78c8566be72416fc0f82b6d991e91f2ad17ad751431f9fa14f31798a524566.jpg)

## July data weak but with some progress

In a quiet week of data, timely survey indicators for July show no very new developments overall but also do not challenge the idea that growth momentum remained firm into 3Q. The REC jobs report reported a 2.2-pt rise in permanent placements to 47.7 (Figure 3), a level that is still low but remains consistent with job growth that is broadly stagnant rather than falling. The PMI sends a brighter message of expansion. The salaries reading stepped down and remains low, but has failed to track wage growth for a while now. Staff availability fell and continues to flag a loosening labour. But the BoE believes the unemployment rate has been broadly steady of late, suggesting the REC may be overstating the pace of loosening.

Figure 3: REC job placements versus LFS employment growth
![](images/13c74b7a5bfdb979d5d49e555ab9742099802c7d7b21af7f698c5951e96e0655.jpg)
Source: ONS, Markit, REC, KPMG

The RICS survey showed some improvement in sales (Figure 4), with expected prices picking up slightly. New buyer enquiries turned positive after three months of decline. But the biggest change came from expected sales, which rose from 22 to 30 and stand at the highest level since the pandemic. This may reflect mortgage rates falling, but in any case suggests confidence with regards to home purchases is picking up slowly.

Figure 4: RICS sales balances
![](images/89fcd01709a3d21aae4df30d05285b3d464584168fe306f523c83a821784f0d7.jpg)

We expect some payback from the prior month’s spike in accommodation prices, which appear to have been affected by the Taylor Swift concerts (although only by 0.1% in the aggregate). This helps to produce a slowing in the year ago rate from 5.7% to 5.5% and pulls the core rate lower. The downward impact would be larger but we assume a lift in airfares in July similar to the summer spike seen in 2023. While energy bills are set to fall 7% in July, an even larger decline last year means the year ago rate of headline inflation will likely rise in this release. The BoE assumes a 5.6% on services inflation, which looks high if the prior month’s accommodation price spike is indeed partly unwound (or if the airfares spike doesn’t occur).

## Sweden and Norway

• Swedish 2Q GDP posts large contraction

• Norway inflation below Norges Bank forecast again...

• ...but dovish pivot next week is very unlikely

Swedish GDP disappointed in the second quarter, contracting 3.2%q/q, saar. We had anticipated a 1% decline while the Riksbank projected flat activity. No details were released with the flash estimate, but monthly indicators suggested a large drag from household consumption whereas net exports dampened the fall. Most likely, inventories were a large negative contributor after a large boost in 1Q. The outcome in 2Q marked the eight consecutive quarter with GDP alternating between growth and contraction.

Survey data have been mixed. The NIER survey declined 1.4pts to 95.0 in July, consistent with GDP rising 0.6%q/q, saar. In contrast, the composite PMI index edged up to 52.5, driven by a solid rise in the service component. The PMIs have previously suffered from seasonality issues in July—as it is in the midst of the Scandinavian summer period—but for what it is worth the level points to GDP rising 0.9%q/q, saar. This is above our forecast of 0.5%, but well-below the Riksbank (1.7%). Details of the surveys were also mixed, with the NIER showing a small fall in price indicators while the PMI counterparts increased. Also, the NIER employment indicators in the fell, but increased in PMI. The overall picture, however, remains that price pressures are muted and employment should rise at a modest pace in 3Q.

Figure 1: Sweden core inflation vs. businesses' CPI expectations
![](images/5fa620968305f24f76cf7e69a040a198e17ccd8537584d4dbf8155d4d14eeac7.jpg)

We expect core inflation to have increased 0.17%m/m (nsa) in July, translating into an annual increase of 2.0%oya. This is two tenths below the Riksbank’s forecast. Correcting for the miss in June, however, our forecast is in line with the bank’s (on a second decimal, our forecast is though 0.08%-pt higher at 2.04%). A sharp SEK depreciation, rising shipping costs, and some payback on services prices (in particular recreation) create some upside risk. The outlook for the remainder of 2H24 looks benign, with core inflation likely to hover around

2%oya (Figure 1). Together with the sharp fall in rates abroad we see the risk skewed towards four cuts in 2H24 vs our call of three. Another potential driver for more cuts is the labor market where we expect a 0.2%-pt rise in the LFS unemployment rate to 8.5% in July.

## Norway: inflation undershoots again

In July, core prices increased 0.77%m/m, nsa, taking the yearago rate down one-tenth to 3.3%oya. The outcome widened the gap to the bank’s forecast from 0.2%-pt to 0.4%-p. Nevertheless, we do not expect a dovish pivot at next week’s Norges Bank meeting where the policy rate is widely expected to be on hold. Instead, we think the recent NOK sell-off will lead to a relatively hawkish rhetoric from the Governor.

Figure 2: Norges Bank core inflation forecast history
![](images/fa28ff29b2ee7c369c44d6114defdbbdf05345efab152fd19cc1d4cf067ca96c.jpg)

Activity data have been weak in recent weeks. The registered unemployment rate increased to 2.1%, one tenth above expectations, while retail sales plummeted 5.1%, the fifth largest decline on record (data since 1979). Although the large fall was mostly a symptom of weather effects and a shorter number of workday—which conversely boosted retail sales by 4.7% in May—the outcome was a disappointment. House prices also contracted as flagged by the leading sales-to-inventory ratio, but against Norges Bank’s expectation of a solid 0.4%m/m increase. This was the second consecutive month with a downward surprise.

The most important data release in the coming weeks is the 2Q GDP estimate. There is more uncertainty than normal about the outcome given Statistics Norway has not published monthly data for April and May (due to “work with the socalled main revision” which is conducted every fifth year). We see GDP rising 0.7%q/q, saar, a notch below Norges Bank’s forecast.

## Emerging Europe

• Israel: Growth likely unimpressive in 2Q, but labor market tightened

• Türkiye: Temporary uptick in inflation

• Hungary: Core CPI stubborn

## Israel: Soft 2Q Growth, tighter labor market

With most of the high-frequency activity data in hand, our assessment is that growth momentum eased significantly in 2Q. In fact, nowcasters based on activity indicators suggest that 2Q momentum could have dropped to around 3%q/q, saar, down from the 14.4% ar bounce recorded in 1Q. However, our labor market conditions index, which aggregates data from a range of relevant indicators (see here), showed significant tightening during 2Q and indicates little slack in the labor market by mid-year (Figure 1). As the changes in LMCI were relatively well correlated with GDP growth in recent years — in part as the statistic offices has used labor market data to proxy activity estimates in select sectors in its early GDP estimates — we take it as a signal that reported GDP growth may be as weak as nowcasters suggest. LMCIbased estimates suggest GDP might have grown around 9% ar last quarter (Figure 2). Our forecast is somewhere in between at 5.75%q/q, saar in 2Q.

Figure 1: Labor market conditions index - Israel
![](images/3010311d7aec00e4246ddc4d0129183d2a16923c071d65655e1dc9ebe9354fb2.jpg)

Figure 2: GDP nowcasters - Israel
![](images/1a33cfcf461d8f466f75a9c2769bc5bc3ff5ce1e0ac1a32886d924e417c7ad0d.jpg)

Another wave of geopolitical tensions in the region may affect growth in 3Q — through the confidence channel, uncertainty raising risk premia, and disruptions to normal functioning of the economy, especially in the northern part of the country. We marginally lowered our 3Q forecast to 5.75%q/q, saar, but suspect that risks might still be skewed to the downside. Our full-year 2024 forecast now stands at 1.6%, down from 1.7% previously. We continue to forecast growth at 4.5% next year.

We also revised our forecast for the policy rate trajectory this past week, following big revisions to our Fed call. Rates in Israel have traditionally been quite sensitive to US rates. However, given the very high geopolitical tensions in the region and increased risk premia in Israeli assets, we think the BoI won’t follow the Fed closely in the near-term. Idiosyncratic inflation drivers related to supply constraints, a tighter labor market and elevated rent inflation will continue to play a role in 2H24, too. Chances of an earlier (October) re-start of the easing cycle in Israel have increased, but we stick to our base case that the next 25bp cut will be delivered in November. That said, a deeper cutting cycle in the US opens space for more significant easing cycle in Israel in 2025. We are adding one more cut to our forecast profile and now see the policy rate at 3.75% by mid-25 vs. 4.0% previously. Risks are skewed toward an even deeper cutting cycle next year, we believe, especially in the case that geopolitical risks are at least partly mitigated by then.

## Türkiye: Temporary uptick in inflation

Headline CPI inflation came in at 3.2% m/m nsa in July, below our expectation of 3.5%, but higher than the market consensus of 3.1%. The downside surprise to our forecast mainly stemmed from fruit, vegetables and clothing prices. Food prices rose 1.8% m/m (JPM forecast 2.5%), bringing oya food inflation down to 58.9% in July from 68.1%. Thanks to real lira appreciation and the slowdown in domestic demand, core goods prices were up only 0.4% m/m in July, 38.3 % oya in July, (56.5% oya in June.) Clothing prices fell 2.7% m/m more than we expected. Services prices rose 4.4% m/m, 85.6% oya, (from 95.3% in June). Rent prices were up 7% m/m (versus 5.1% in June), while restaurant & hotel prices were up 3.3% m/m (versus 3.1% in June). Core inflation (C Index; CPI excluding energy, food, beverages, tobacco and gold) rose 2.5% m/m in July (versus 1.7% m/m in June), leading oya core inflation down to 60.2% in July, versus 71.4% in June (Figure 3).

Headline inflation fell to 61.8% oya in July, (71.6% in June) on the back of strong favourable base effects. Energy prices rose sharply by 10% m/m due to the electricity price hike of 38% and motor fuel price hikes on the back of automatic tax adjustments. That being said, energy inflation went down to 80.1% oya in July, from 84.6% on the back of strong base effects.

Core inflation momentum (C index, 3m change, saar, %) fell to 33.5% in July, from 37.5% thanks to core goods prices. That said, seasonally-adjusted core inflation (C index) increased to 2.3% m/m in July, from 1.8% in June due to services prices, particularly rent prices. Despite a monthly uptick in July, 3-month core inflation momentum continued to ease. The improvement in services inflation momentum remained subdued relative to core goods momentum. We expect core inflation momentum to improve further through core goods in 2H24 thanks to real lira appreciation and the slowdown in economic activity.

State-owned gas supplier BOTAS hiked the natural gas prices by 38%. Energy watchdog EMRA stated that it will result in an average 24.4% increase in final natural gas prices for households nationwide. The weight of natural gas in the CPI basket is 2.57%, which means a 0.63%-pt direct impact on August inflation. We expect motor fuel prices to fall in August thanks to the decline in Brent crude oil prices. Food inflation is likely to be lower in August as well. As a result, we expect monthly inflation to fall towards 2.2% or below in August, from 3.2% m/m in July.

We expect headline inflation to fall sharply towards 51.6% in August and 42.5% by year-end (Figure 4). We assume there will be no utility price hikes this year. Leading indicators signal a slowdown in domestic demand in 3Q24. We will monitor to what extent real appreciation of lira and slowdown in the economic activity will offset the natural gas price hike this month coupled with the effects of automatic interim wage hikes for pensioners and civil servants. We maintain our end-2024 inflation forecast at 42.5% oya and end-2025 inflation forecast at 25% oya. We believe that the risks around our inflation forecasts are balanced now.

Figure 3: Turkey Consumer Prices
![](images/9e51523d583092f0cab28da6ee8b6702a6898bfbbc5e2603fb023d97e6e5d202.jpg)

![](images/06df3b97046fb630e90988bae099c4359f70d71cefb748c107bd2a895b54dba8.jpg)
