## Hungary: Core CPI stubborn

Headline inflation accelerated to 4.1%oya in July (Figure 5), from 3.7%, the first reading outside the NBH’s target band this year (even if still very close to it), beating market expectations marginally on the upside (Consensus and J.P. Morgan: 4%oya).

Non-core items behaved close to expectations. Food prices were up 0.5%m/m, in line with our forecast, reflecting in large part the end of government-enforced mandatory discounting in basic foods sold in retail. This, together with an unfavourable base, resulted in a jump in the over-year-ago comparison to 0.3%oya, from -1.8%. Fuel prices were up 3.8%m/m, slightly more than the 2.9% indicated in the weekly surveys, while regulated prices surprised in the opposite direction, falling 0.1%m/m (we expected +0.1%m/m).

Measures of core inflation confirmed the exhaustion of disinflation dynamics that was visible in the data already last month. The NBH’s main metric, so called tax-adjusted core jumped to 4.7%oya from 4.1% (Figure 6), a decimal above our forecast. The alternative measure, which excludes processed food from tax-adjusted core (and which we prefer), picked up to 5.7%oya, from 5.6%, whereas we predicted a decline of one-tenth. This marks the first increase in this core CPI metric after 18 months of consecutive declines. In momentum terms, this implies stickiness around the 6% annualised pace area, which suggests upward pressure on both measures of inflation and is clearly too high for the NBH to meet the target sustainably.

The sticky core inflation dynamics back the case for an NBH pause. After delivering a total of 1125 basis points of easing, the NBH has signalled the scope for additional easing in 2H24 is limited, with space for perhaps a couple more 25bp cuts. We have taken this to mean a more spaced out easing approach, with cuts delivered at quarterly meetings backed by new inflation reports (September and December this year). In our view, the space created by the shift in market expectations for major CB rates is offset by the inflation picture, the path of EUR/HUF has not benefitted much from lower expected Fed rates, and remains too close to 400 for the NBH to relax, at least not with this level of inflation. We continue therefore to expect a pause later this month, followed by cuts in Sep/ Dec, leaving the year-end rate at 6.25%.

SARB official reserves

## South Africa

• SARB to remain focused on SA inflation outlook, which continues to improve at the margin

• We now see a earlier return to neutral stance by 1Q25 (previously 2Q25)...

• ...with a 40% - 50% chance of a 50bp move in either September or November

While the SARB remains primarily focused on the local inflation outlook, the pace of the global easing cycle and the terms-of-trade are relevant, particularly for the inflation risk assessment. In our view, the likelihood of a larger than our previously forecasted 25bp cut in September has now risen to 40% - 50%, but FX pressures and services inflation risks will determine the eventuality. We now expect an earlier return to a neutral stance already by 1Q25 (previously mid-25).

Figure 1: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/25422244b067bb6200eebcd961020deb60902f94e376c9a0035682b0ad4dd5e7.jpg)
Source: StatsSA, J.P. Morgan forecast

In recent months, the SARB had remained cautious and hesitant to start an easing cycle, even as local political and fiscal risks diminished. A key argument had been that inflation risks were skewed to the upside, including due to potential FX passthrough. To be sure, the outlook for the currency remains uncertain, and tied to terms-of-trade and global risk appetite, yet the currency has so far remained largely range-bound. We think services inflation has surprised to the downside and upside core inflation risks have reduced. Moreover, the nearterm outlook for non-core inflation (particularly transport inflation) has improved further, which may trim headline inflation to 0.5%pt below target next quarter (Figure 1).

The SARB is likely to update its inflation risk assessment to more balanced at the next MPC meeting, and perhaps revise down its inflation outlook by 0.2%pt near-term. With headline inflation likely undershooting its target from September and activity soft, the monetary policy stance is about 100bp tighter than warranted by growth-inflation dynamics. While we think it now becomes a close call for a 50 vs. 25bp cut in September (or alternatively in November), much will hinge on the confirmation of softer services inflation and USD/ZAR in coming weeks to build the SARB’s confidence of a more balanced inflation outlook.

## Australia and New Zealand

• The RBA kept the policy rate unchanged at 4.35%

But the guidance was hawkish and the Bank upgraded its core inflation forecasts

• We expect Australia’s July unemployment rate (data next week) to remain at 4.1%

• We anticipate the RBNZ will cut 25bp at next week’s meeting

The RBA met this week amidst ongoing volatility from the weaker-than-anticipated US non-farm payrolls release. The board left the cash rate steady at 4.35% as expected. The statement, finalized on Tuesday, read as if it could have been prepared a week prior and underplayed any recent changes in the US/global backdrop to focus on domestic issues.

The board signalled that its focus remains on domestic inflation, and it isn’t as enthusiastic as markets have been with respect to progress in the last CPI print. In the concluding sections, the board note "data have reinforced the need to remain vigilant to upside risks to inflation", a clear pushback to pricing of cuts in the front 2-3 meetings. Governor Bullock also stated several times in the press conference that “a nearterm reduction in the cash rate doesn’t align with the board’s current thinking”, and that this week’s decision was between a hike and a hold.

The leadership will "continue to pay close attention to developments in the global economy and financial markets", an acknowledgement of recent volatility, but only in passing. In a question about peer pressure to follow the global pack, Bullock argued “the point of a floating exchange rate is you do have flexibility” to not be pegged to other central banks. This is clearly a green light to price some spread widening to US, at least near-term.

The board argues "policy will need to be sufficiently restrictive” until it “is confident that inflation is moving sustainably towards the target range." The balance in this comment leaves us comfortable with our call for a first easing in Feb 2025: the leadership doesn't need annual inflation in the target band to start easing ("moving sustainably towards ...") but with policy starting less restrictive than others (e.g. Fed, RBNZ) the hurdle to start is still higher than other central banks.

## NZ labor market continues to soften

Turning to NZ, the activity data continue to soften, with the unemployment rate up to 4.6%, in line with our forecast (and the RBNZ's), but a shade below the Bloomberg consensus at 4.7%. The 1Q result was revised up a tenth to 4.4%. The unemployment rate has now risen 1%-pt over a year and

1.4%-pt from the trough. Recent business survey readings on GDP/activity and hiring suggest further increases are likely (Figure 1).

Figure 1: NZ Unemployment and NZIER survey labour constraint
![](images/66a82109425866f40bf7c3db8b82c72cf75c1872b1b91bf00f10b058cc692c01.jpg)
Source: Stats NZ, NZIER, J.P. Morgan.

Employment growth was not far from our expectations in 2Q at +0.4%q/q, with another rise in participation (71.7%) raising the implied job creation at the given unemployment rate. That headline employment/income story wasn't, however, matched in the broader details. Hours worked fell sharply in both the household survey (-1.2%q/q) and the QES (firms survey, -0.9%q/q). The QES measure of employment also fell well short of the employment metric in the household survey, with filled jobs contracting (-)0.5%q/q and full-time equivalent positions off (-0.8%q/q). This lends a much softer undertone, which aligns with the business surveys and the consistent fall in job advertisements, the latter having eased back to decade lows.

## RBNZ to kick off the cutting cycle

While the RBA continues to weigh up the merits of hiking vs holding, we expect the RBNZ to cut 25bp next week. The cut reflects likely achievement of the inflation target in the CPI result, and concerning growth/labor market performance. After this week’s data, the unemployment rate is up 1.4%-pt from the trough. Leading business survey indicators of labor demand and excess capacity – which have been reliable this cycle – suggest upside risks to the RBNZ’s forecast of peak unemployment rate in the low 5s.

The policy rate is very restrictive and the RBNZ’s strategy of targeting the data rather than its forecast leaves it potentially behind the curve. As an economy with high interest rate sensitivity, the cost of this strategy has been relatively high. And with an apparent break into deeper contraction implied by recent PMIs (Figure 2), concerted action is required sooner rather than later. The committee appeared to pivot last meeting, flagging that “the extent of this restraint will be tempered over time consistent with the expected decline in inflation pressures”. The “expected” here suggests the committee no longer needs to see the rollover in %oya rates.

Figure 2: NZ GDP and composite PMI
![](images/fc51691d69624bd8f649c756c6de315bc5a422fc1a9f5ef1450e5c8ea2033c2d.jpg)

In the commentary, we expect the committee will emphasize the downshift in core, and in inflation expectations/pricing intentions metrics to underscore this argument. Global developments also lean dovish for a central bank which has arguably held policy steady for longer in order to move with the pack of other central banks.

## Australia

## Data releases and forecasts

Week of Aug 12 - 16
<table><tr><td>Tue</td><td>Wage price index</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>23Q3</td><td>23Q4</td><td>24Q1</td><td>24Q2</td></tr><tr><td>Aug 13 11:30am</td><td>%q/q, sa</td><td>1.2</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.8</td></tr></table>

We forecast quarterly wage growth increased 0.8%q/q in the June quarter, an outcome consistent with the annual rate decelerating to 3.8%oya. The unemployment rate crept modestly higher in 2Q as the lagged effect of prior monetary policy tightening weighed on labor demand. Rising unemployment will be a headwind to aggregate wage growth, particularly across those industries where employment contracts are set via short-term individual arrangements.

Collective agreement wage growth is likely to prove more resilient in the near term given longer contract durations, with the tracking data indicating wages set via this method are still running near 4%oya. The impulse from Award/minimum wages to the WCI are highly seasonal and will only become evident from July, though we flag this support will be smaller than in the past two years given the deceleration in legislated wage growth.

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Wed Labor force survey</td><td colspan="4"></td></tr><tr><td>Aug 15</td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">11:30am</td><td>Unemployment rate (%), sa</td><td>4.1</td><td>4.0</td><td>4.1</td><td>4.1</td></tr><tr><td>Employment (ch. 000s), sa</td><td>36.1</td><td>39.5</td><td>50.2</td><td>15</td></tr><tr><td>Participation rate (%), sa</td><td>66.8</td><td>66.8</td><td>66.9</td><td>66.9</td></tr></table>

Australia’s unemployment rate increased one-tenth to 4.1% in June, returning to the top of the 2024 range and now 0.6%-pts above the cycle low (June 2023: 3.5%). While we expect the jobless rate to drift higher over time, we forecast stability in the coming week’s print with employment growth forecast to increase +15K. As always, labor supply remains the swing factor (we expect the participation rate to hold steady at 66.9%) with an unexpected increase (decrease) likely to deliver a larger (smaller) than anticipated employment change.

## Greater China

• China: slowing exports amid concerns on softening global manufacturing trend

CPI inflation ticked up to 0.5%oya on food prices; core CPI remained soft; look for further rate cuts

Taiwan: July CPI inflation ticked up, partly reflecting typhoon impact; expect stable policy rates in 2H

• July export activity moderated from earlier strength; tech demand remained solid

• Next week: China IP, FAI, retail sales, credit

Since the beginning of the year, the PBOC has faced a policy dilemma between price and currency stability, and between growth and financial stability concerns. Inadequate domestic demand growth and deflation pressures called for rate cuts, but CNY depreciation pressure amid interest rate differentials created the imperative for more stable interest rates. The repricing of the Fed rate outlook and the softening of the USD means currency stability may no longer be a binding constraint. In the past two weeks, for example, the CNY has appreciated almost 1.7% against the USD, and the PBOC has taken the opportunity to restart rate cuts in late July, including a 10bp cut in 7-day reverse repo rate and LPR, a 20bp cut in 1-year MLF and a simultaneous reduction in deposit rates.

Based on the new Fed rate outlook, we expect the PBOC to continue to cut rates and we add a 10 bps cut in 4Q in addition to the 10 bps cut we had penciled for 1Q25, both aimed at lifting growth and mitigating deflationary pressure in the economy. In addition, our forecasts look for another 25bp RRR cut in 3Q, though TSF growth will likely remain soft at 8.1% by the year-end (vs. 9.5% last year), with bank loan growth at 8.4% (vs. 10.6% last year).

## Export activity slowed in July

China’s July exports came in below expectations, rising 7.0%oya, translating to a sequential decline of 4.0% m/m sa (or contraction of 2.5% 3m/3m saar, Figure 1). Nominal imports rose 7.2%oya in July, or falling 1.5% m/m sa, adding to the decline of 3.1% m/m sa in June. The trade surplus registered US\$84.6 bn in July, down from the historical-high level of \$99.0 bn in June.

The export sector has been an important growth driver for the Chinese economy in recent quarters amid a slowing housing market and sluggish consumption growth. The slowing in July exports, amid concerns of easing global manufacturing sector activity, sends tentative signals that the export engine may soften going into 2H, reinforcing the need for supportive domestic macro policy to stabilize near-term growth.

Chinese exporters’ diversification efforts across products and destinations may continue to support export performance in the near term. However, the potential rise of protectionism, wider use of tariffs, export controls, investment restrictions and sanctions, may lead to further distortion or disruption to the global trade system and exert pressure on macro growth.

Figure 1: China merchandise exports and imports
![](images/5ba275602f6dea7a68cca4d5d40b1667238293ded33360b50438842168e72b78.jpg)

## July CPI inflation ticked up on food prices

China’s July CPI inflation rate picked up to 0.5%oya (or 0.2% m/m sa), compared to 0.2%oya in June (Figure 2). Taking the first seven months together, headline CPI inflation has averaged 0.2%oya. Meanwhile, core CPI inflation remained soft, rising 0.4%oya in July (or 0.0% m/m sa), compared to 0.6%oya in June. Food prices appear to be stabilizing (with recent uptick in pork prices), and energy prices have moved along with global commodity prices, with volatility in travel and transport costs along with holiday season (prices for recreational and cultural services picked up 0.4% m/m sa in July amid the summer travel season). Aside from these factors, general pricing power for goods and services seems to have remained soft amid sluggish domestic demand growth and weak private sector confidence.

Figure 2: China's inflation dynamics
![](images/7e620618d7a092789f6f92cba4fde848d03b23a9fcd3ac2e04beb6210e6f0a0f.jpg)

Headline PPI fell 0.8%oya in July (unchanged from -0.8%oya in June). In sequential terms, PPI edged up 0.1% m/m sa in July (third consecutive monthly increase in sequential terms). Details suggest easing trends in prices of industrial metals and construction-related materials, with a modest increase in energy-related PPI.

Overall, we expect the low inflation environment to stay through 2H24,while a moderate recovery in domestic pork prices and a low-base effect from last year would help to avoid the return of CPI deflation in 2H24. Meanwhile, demand-supply imbalance and excess capacity concerns remain significant. Our forecast for full-year 2024 CPI inflation stands at 0.4%, while PPI will likely stay in deflation territory (in %oya terms) through the year-end.

## Taiwan: July CPI inflation ticked up

Taiwan’s CPI inflation came in line with expectations in July, as headline CPI rose 2.52%oya, compared to 2.42%oya in June (Figure 3). Our seasonal adjustment process shows headline CPI rose 0.2% m/m sa in July, following the increase of 0.3% m/m sa in June. Further details suggest a moderate pick-up in food prices (partly reflecting temporary typhoon effect), stable energy prices, and a steady rise in core CPI. Meanwhile, core CPI rose at a steady pace of 1.84%oya in July, compared with 1.83%oya in June, translating to a rise of 0.2% m/m sa (vs +0.2% m/m sa in June). In addition, general service prices rose 2.51%oya in July (vs 2.52%oya in June), or up 0.3% m/m sa (vs 0.3% m/m sa in June). We expect Taiwan’s headline CPI inflation to stay modestly above 2%oya in August, and to ease below 2% towards 4Q.

Figure 3: Taiwan headline and core CPI
![](images/2047560e808e8c96771314d28e3ba9f98960e689cb295c24450fa4a56c68744d.jpg)
Source: DGBAS, J..P. Morgan

Our baseline forecast looks for Taiwan’s central bank to keep policy rates on hold through 2H24 as policymakers watch for signs of further easing in CPI inflation. Note that core CPI remained steady at 1.8%oya in July, despite the temporary typhoon-boost to food prices. In addition, the external demand backdrop has turned darker lately, with easing in the global manufacturing PMI in the past two months, along with concerns regarding labor market conditions in the US. As such, besides the focus on the domestic inflation path, the Taiwan central bank may need to attend to growth risks going into 2H.

## Export activity moderated

Following upside surprise in June, Taiwan’s export activity moderated in July, broadly in line with expectations. Merchandise exports grew 3.1%oya in July. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that exports (in volume terms) fell 3.9% m/m sa in July, following the bounce of 5.9% m/m sa in June. Overall tech exports remained solid (Figure 4), rising further by 2.2% m/m sa in July (adding to the rise of 6.0% m/m sa in June), including a further solid gain in exports of information and communication products (reflecting AI-related exports) by 6.3% m/m sa. Non-tech exports fell 9.9% m/m sa in July, partly reflecting the typhoon disruption during the month, following the gain of 7.2% m/m sa in June.

Figure 4: Taiwan export breakdown by products - level US\$ bn, sa, 3mma
![](images/d0d4f85ee84cf7342c62371f1208104de381dcca2cf0464ef2c2397b20f74b62.jpg)
Overall, our baseline forecasts continue to look for a steady uptrend in Taiwan’s tech exports in the coming quarters. Meanwhile, we reckon that uncertainty on the global economy’s outlook seems to be on the rise, giving rise to potential risk factors to external demand for Taiwan exporters going forward.

Merchandise trade (7 Aug)
<table><tr><td colspan="5">US$ bn</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Balance</td><td>82.6</td><td>99.0</td><td>95.1</td><td>84.6</td></tr><tr><td>Exports</td><td>302.3</td><td>307.9</td><td>307.7</td><td>300.6</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>7.6</td><td>8.6</td><td>96</td><td>7.0</td></tr><tr><td>Imports</td><td>219.7</td><td>208.8</td><td>212.6</td><td>215.9</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>1.8</td><td>-2.3</td><td>56</td><td>7.2</td></tr></table>

By product breakdown, exports of low-end consumer goods fell 6.9% m/m sa in July (or down 21.2% 3m3/m saar) in July. Exports of mechanical & electrical products fell 1.5% m/m, sa, while exports of high-tech products rose 0.9% m/m sa. Auto exports rose 1.5% m/m sa after easing in recent months. By destination, the slowing in July exports has been rather broad-based. Exports to DM markets generally slowed, including exports to the US (-2.0% m/m sa), Europe (-3.1% m/m sa) and Japan (-3.9% m/m sa). Exports to EM Asia fell by 5.9% m/m sa, along with slowing in exports to other EM markets (LatAm: -1.8%, Africa: -3.1%).

Imports of commodities generally came in on the soft side, with a decline in imports of crude oil and coal. Regarding imports of major industrial metals, imports of iron ore and copper both weakened in July. Overall, the latest signals from the import front seem to suggest lingering domestic demand softness.

## FX reserves (7 Aug)

<table><tr><td colspan="4">US$ bn</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>FX reserves</td><td>3232</td><td>3222</td><td>3270 3256</td></tr></table>

China’s July FX reserves rose US\$34bn to \$3,256.4bn, below our expectation of US\$3270bn. This follows the \$9.7bn modest fall in June. Gold reserves remained unchanged, after solid increases in the previous 18 consecutive months through April. The July trade report disappointed, with softer exports and stronger imports, leading to a narrowing merchandise trade surplus at US\$84.6bn (vs. \$95.1bn in our forecast). Services account has roughly recovered to the pre-pandemic level, averaging at a US\$30.1bn deficit in 2Q. Hence, we expect current account surplus for goods and services may have narrowed to \$34.6bn (vs \$40.7bn in June). Meanwhile, the US dollar index eased from 105.9 to 104.1 in July, leading to an estimated currency valuation gain of US\$22.6bn. Taken together, we tentatively estimate a US\$23.3bn implied capital outflow in July (vs US\$-34.8bn in June).

Food prices rose 0.4% m/m sa in July (or up 4.6%oya), as vegetable prices jumped 9.3% m/m nsa on typhoon effect. Besides, the aggregate energy-related CPI component edged up 0.1%m/m sa (or up 2.5%oya).

## Korea

• We expect an earlier but not deeper nor earlier BoK cut cycle

• Current account surplus widened in June with seasonality boost

Without a major data release this week, financial markets focused on the possible impacts from changes in U.S. Fed policy rate paths on Bank of Korea policy. We have re-evaluated and now we expect the BoK to reach the same terminal rate two quarters earlier than the previous forecast. But in our view, the likelihood of a surprise cut in August and/or deeper policy rate cut cycle is limited, given the lingering concern over household leverage.

## Expecting earlier but not deeper rate cuts

In our view, the current tightening stance appears to have maintained an appropriate level to facilitate a soft landing for the macro economy following the pandemic shock. While 2Q24 GDP contracted (following an unsustainably strong 1Q) with weak readings across domestic demand, there remains a trend recovery in overall growth impulses, and we continue to forecast a rise in annual GDP growth to 2.7% y/y this year compared to 1.4% in 2023. As such, our estimate of the output gap is tracking modestly positive, amid gradually stabilizing inflation due to the unwinding of supply-side shocks, which implies only a gradual normalization of the monetary policy stance towards the neutral range.

In addition to growth and inflation, the BoK has cited Korea’s exceptionally elevated household leverage level as a factor justifying a cautious approach in neutralizing the monetary policy stance. Notably, in the July MPC meeting communication and minutes, the MPC members were more hawkish than market expectations for the meeting, as most members saw higher risks from early easing of the policy stance potentially re-igniting household leverage growth. If we adhere to the BoK’s current signaling and risk bias on household leverage growth, there has been even a risk that the expected policy rate cut may be delayed (we had expected 4Q24 for the first rate cut timing, more likely in October than in November).

That said, as expectations for the U.S. Fed policy rate path change amid rising concerns over external growth conditions, there may be an increased possibility of earlier and/or faster policy rate cuts. In our revised behavioral Taylor rule estimates (Figure 1) without major changes in the output gap and inflation expectations but with higher household credit growth, there is a tendency for the BoK to act faster than our previous expectation. However, the terminal policy rate level remains broadly unchanged, with mixed contributions from household leverage issues versus the external environment,

including the US Fed’s policy path. Reflecting the updated conditions and estimation for reaction, we now see 25bp cuts in 4Q24, 1Q25 and 2Q25 to reach the same terminal rate (2.75%) two quarters faster than previous expectations (previously expected 25bp cuts in 4Q24, 2Q25 and 4Q25). Nonetheless, we remain mindful of the downside risk to the growth outlook given the volatile financial conditions, and the policy rate path should depend on possible changes in macroeconomic fundamentals.

Figure 1: Taylor rule and policy rate
![](images/083620c3aab716d1f2d1bf5e9574000c92038c0a89fe7c5ee7a3c13d5d2f0411.jpg)

## CA surplus widened in June

The current account surplus widened sharply in June to US\$12.3bn with a stronger-than-expected seasonal boost from the US\$8.9bn surplus in May. The seasonally adjusted balance recorded US\$8.6bn sa (vs. US\$3.6bn in April and US\$8.6bn in May), to continue the gradual recovery trend. While the current account surplus recovery continues to imply a stronger buffer to external demand or financial shocks, the immediate implication for financial markets may be limited, as financial outflows (albeit it is mostly recycling of the current account surplus) also increase to offset the impact on the FX reserve accumulation.

Figure 2: Korea current account breakdown
![](images/989e05fc97963bdbdcbfa6d0a07c37a265a075df156ed10dda8de0893296fb8a.jpg)

In seasonally adjusted terms, the merchandise and service balance broadly recovered while the primary income surplus decreased (Figure 2). The merchandise trade surplus widened to US\$9.7bn sa from US\$5.1bn in April and US\$8.3bn in May, likely along the terms of trade stabilization and exports volume recovery. The service balance deficit narrowed to US\$1.6bn in June after peaking at US\$3.0bn in March, as the transportation balance modestly recovered and travel balance deficit narrowed. Meanwhile, the primary income surplus narrowed to US\$0.8bn from US\$2.5bn in May. In trends, the primary income surplus is gradually rising through monthly volatility to reflect the larger foreign investment position, yet the initial positive impact from the tax benefit (for re-shoring of foreign earnings) in early 2023 has eased.

In the financial account, the net FDI outflow held up at US\$5.3bn nsa and net portfolio outflow increased to US\$9.0bn from US\$4.8bn in May, mainly as locals increased foreign equity investment amid foreigners withdrawing US\$4.6bn from domestic bond securities. In the cumulative 1H24, the 1H current account surplus was greater than the full-year surplus in 2022 and 2023, yet the financial outflow also outpaced the annual figure in 2023 with a decrease in foreign inflows on bonds investments. The FX reserve was broadly unchanged in June, yet decreased US\$5.9bn in 1H cumulatively.

## ASEAN

• Forecasts for larger Fed rate cuts opens the door for tactical cuts from BSP and BI

In a US recession (not base case), ASEAN exports would be adversely affected, with domestic demand spillovers

• Sharply lower commodity prices provide support to external accounts, allowing for deeper rate cuts

The dovish re-pricing of the Fed policy path in a no-recession scenario provides the green light for tactical cuts for some ASEAN central banks (e.g., BSP, BI). Although not our base case, we explore the macro implications of a US-led downturn. Given high sensitivity to US capex, ASEAN exports would be adversely affected, with spillovers into domestic demand and growth, but weaker commodity prices would also support external positions, allowing deeper and more meaningful policy easing.

## Green light for measured, tactical cuts

Last week’s weak US jobs data and the subsequent risk-off behavior in financial markets have raised questions about the global “soft landing” scenario. At this juncture, the JPM view is that although an imminent recession is unlikely, the fasterthan-expected slowdown in the US labor market will compel the Fed to accelerate the normalization of currently restrictive policy settings (i.e., 250bps of rate cuts by mid-2025) to safeguard the US expansion.

In this scenario, the response from ASEAN central banks (CBs) will likely be differentiated (Figure 1), as we argued in an earlier note (see link). CBs that are sensitive to external financial conditions (i.e., core rates, US\$) with restrictive policy stances (i.e., high ex-ante real rates), such as BSP and BI, are in position to “follow the Fed”, although we think the latter has less scope to cut due to offsetting drags from weaker commodity prices and foreign portfolio outflows in equities.

On the other hand, CBs with less Fed-driven reaction functions, such as BoT and BNM, may not be inclined to ease prematurely, especially if the global business cycle expansion is intact.

There are also country-specific reasons against near-term easing. For Thailand, elevated household debt and pro-cyclical fiscal stimulus (e.g., the digital wallet scheme in 4Q24) will likely keep the BoT on a cautious footing. For Malaysia, recent demand-side measures (e.g., civil service pay hike, EPF withdrawal scheme) and fuel subsidy rationalization plans could bring upside risk to growth and inflation in the next 6-9 months. To the extent that the recent MYR strength is de-facto tightening for exporters, BNM has other tools (e.g., US\$ buying in spot and forward market) to manage FX appreciation pressures instead of resorting to rate cuts.

Figure 1: ASEAN policy rate
![](images/f02399ad002d393deeb5759ebd0dea15fce2e3611607f79077eca03bbcffc95e.jpg)

## No harm asking – what if?

Although not the base case, it is worth asking what the macro implications are in a global recession scenario. Historically, the primary driver of ASEAN exports is the US capex cycle (Figure 2). As such, the first order impact of a US-emanated growth shock would be significant declines in exports, manufacturing, and investment activity. In fact, we would contend that the spillovers of a US-led recession this time could be bigger, given that US business capex has become more import-intensive vis-à-vis private consumption in recent years (Figure 3).

Figure 2: US durable goods orders vs. ASEAN exports %q/q, saar, both scales, US\$ terms
![](images/406054d5005b87386962f60e87edbf59688b9d3223ea467a3014832f3d2bc873.jpg)
Figure 3: US import intensity

% of private non-residential equipment capex
![](images/4e72d90855b8b190a0780bb73cc978a109b577916f16cbf0f66f964a9635bfe6.jpg)

We develop a simple analysis to assess the vulnerability of ASEAN economies in a US-led trade shock (Figure 4). Vietnam jumps out of the page in terms of total export exposure to the US, but almost all other economies exhibit high sensitivity to US capex, likely reflecting a trade relationship with the US that is highly cyclical (e.g., autos, appliances, electronics, machinery). The total trade exposure to the US is conceivably higher using a global value chain (GVC) approach, where we add exports of intermediate goods that are reprocessed elsewhere (e.g., China, Taiwan) and shipped to end-consumers in DM.

Figure 4: ASEAN exports to US Exports to US, % of GDP
![](images/947b8c9f55eeca6adf72f3ce70220fc8198e16ff4a3c175f066dab41624c6bad.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan estimates 1. Calculated in %q/q, saar terms in 2010-23 period

The impact of a sharp deceleration in trade on growth can also be exacerbated by de-stocking activity. Our analysis of recent manufacturing PMI prints show that excess inventories is an issue most in Indonesia and least in Vietnam (Figure 5).

Figure 5: ASEAN mfg PMI - finished goods inventory Z-score¹
![](images/437346c52ad8108f969b3e67546c99c9ef03d39ec1ab77aba85c11c9511b197a.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, J.P. Morgan estimates 1. Calculated over 2016-23 period

## Stronger external balances

Recessions are also associated with a sharp drop in commodity prices as demand for cyclicals saps. For ASEAN, this scenario constitutes a positive terms-of-trade shock that strength ens the current account (CA) position (Figure 6). Interestingly, except Indonesia, the region has become a bigger importer of commodities since the pandemic (Table 1). Malaysia, for example, has become a net importer of crude oil and refined petroleum (Figure 7), a major reversal from the pre-pandemic status as a net oil exporter.

Figure 6: Commodity prices vs. ASEAN¹ CA balance
![](images/e089e3681a3365b0a3e849adb5467ee35a61f5909c793f5ec9dccb994b049fc8.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan, shaded regions denote previous recessions 1. Simple aveage of ID, MY, TH, PH

Besides better terms-of-trade from lower commodity prices, CA improvement during recessions comes from import compression due to weaker domestic demand. The combination of weaker GDP, stronger CA position, and DM policy easing typically override FX weakness from foreign portfolio outflows and create the conditions for ASEAN central banks to lower policy rates meaningfully to stimulative territory.

## India

• RBI maintains status quo

• Inflation, growth, unsecured lending, Fed easing all support potential RBI easing

• Next Week: July inflation at 3.6% oya

RBI kept its policy rate unchanged alongside retaining “withdrawal of accommodation” stance as it remains committed to align inflation to its 4% target. While RBI revised its 2Q24 GDP forecast a tad lower we believe the risks are tilted to the downside given the corporate results as well as some high frequency indicators like core IP. We also expect CPI inflation to be lower than RBI’s 3Q inflation forecasts. This in addition to expectations of Fed easing on the horizon, as well as a gradual slowing in unsecured lending, raises the likelihood of RBI easing in 4Q24.

## RBI expectedly stays on hold

The decision to hold reflected another 4-2 vote. The tone of the statement was cautious and similar to previous meetings, which was expected given recent turmoil in financial markets and pressure on some emerging market currencies, including the Rupee. The MPC made two changes to their near-term forecasts. Firstly, 2Q GDP growth (April-June) was revised down from 7.3% oya to 7.1% because, as the Governor’s speech noted, recent high frequency data (corporate profitability, government expenditure and core industries output) were lower than anticipated. The full-year GDP forecast for 2024-25, however, was retained at 7.2%. Secondly, headline CPI for the current quarter (July-September) was increased markedly from 3.8% to 4.4% on higher food prices (Figure 1), even as full-year CPI inflation is retained at 4.5%.

Figure 1: Food inflation
![](images/67858b1c069012fbbd6b8e67462668c1d44048f5867fa3fcb9cc11b0b0d001ae.jpg)

## Preconditions for RBI easing emerging

We had recently brought forward and added monetary easing in our forecast for India, prompted by the growth scare in the U.S. and the likelihood of the Fed easing more sharply in the months to come. The RBI policy call is contingent on four factors: growth slowing below what is commonly presumed, more confirmation that headline CPI is settling below 5%, concerns about unsecured lending and financial exuberance being progressively alleviated, and a global environment that is conducive to EM easing. We believe all these factors are in motion.

Even as the RBI has reduced its 2Q GDP forecast to 7.1%oya, we worry that the risks are to the downside, given the slowing of corporate earnings (Figure 2) and some other high frequency data. We expect 2Q GDP to print below 7%. And with the monsoon progressing well food prices have inflected in August, earlier then we had envisaged. We expect July CPI to come in at 3.6%. Current quarter CPI would hence average below 4%, less than the RBI’s forecast of 4.4%. Furthermore, unsecured lending has been gradually slowing, although growth in unsecured loans like credit cards, despite slowing, remains elevated at 23% oya in June (Figure 3). Lastly, if the Fed were to move 125 bps over the rest of the year, as we currently expect, without the U.S. going into recession, the global backdrop would be much more primed for EM easing.

Figure 2: Earnings of Private Manufacturing Listed Firms
![](images/3cc6de613d83506136469727661ad60a0fb16206aa5843064241a322f0b8894c.jpg)

Figure 3: Unsecured Loan Growth by Banks
![](images/469ef016446fd3d4e3f73cb61c6ddc4ce9b81c4ceb89f1ea18a73b7e7360398b.jpg)
This is the backdrop against which we expect the RBI to ease later this year, even as their current tone remains cautious. We expect the first cut at the October review, by which time the Fed would have eased, and more will be known about GDP growth and food inflation. But we recognize the RBI could also wait till December if it wants more confirmation of the aforementioned criteria.

## Data releases and forecasts Week of August 12-16

<table><tr><td>Fri</td><td colspan="5">Consumer prices</td></tr><tr><td>12-Aug</td><td>% oya</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>CPI</td><td>4.8</td><td>4.8</td><td>5.1</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Core-Core</td><td>3.4</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.4</td><td>3.7</td></tr></table>

CPI inflation is expected to soften from 5.1%oya last month on very favorable base effects. In sequential terms, headline prices likely rose by a strong 0.9% m/m, sa in July led by food prices which strengthened 1.2%. The increase in food prices was led by volatile vegetables. The good news is that high frequency prices suggest that vegetable prices, which saw a sharp jump in the last couple of months, have begun normalizing in August. Core prices are likely to rise sharply in July reflecting mobile tariff hikes. We expect core-core inflation to come at 3.7% oya in July rising from 3.4% in June.

<table><tr><td>Fri</td><td colspan="5">Industrial Produc-</td></tr><tr><td>tion 12-Aug % oya</td><td colspan="5"></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2"></td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>% oya</td><td>5.4</td><td>5.0</td><td>5.9</td><td>5.4</td></tr></table>

We look for IP to rise 0.5% m/m, sa in sequential terms on the back of 1.4% gains in June. The increase in July is expected to driven by the mining sector with coal production witnessing a sharp jump.

## Asia Focus: Asset and wealth management

Hong Kong’s Securities and Futures Commission (SFC) recently published the 2023 Asset and Wealth Management Activities survey. The total AUM of the asset and wealth management (AWM) business grew 2.1%y/y to HK\$31.2 trillion in 2023, about 10.5% of the nominal GDP (vs. 10.9% in 2022). Compared with 2022, the investor base (excluding REITs and assets held under trusts attributable to non-LCs/ RIs) remained diversified (Figure 1), with 36% from HK SAR, 10% from mainland China, and 24% from North America, largely the same as in previous years. The asset management and fund advisory business recorded an AUM of HK\$23,060 billion by end-2023, with assets managed in HK accounting for 54.6% of the AUM of the asset management business (excl. fund advisory business and REITs).

Figure 1: HK asset and wealth managment AUM by investor base
![](images/183e7ea35d36faac1b3c31a612cceb2207a804921efe9dff8f4f16982b2a1177.jpg)
Note: Excl. REITs and assets held under trusts attributable to non-LCs/RIs.

Several developments are notable. First, the share of AUM invested in mainland China has declined from 22% of those managed in HK in 2022 to 17% in 2023 (Figure 2), which is likely due to the poor performance in China’s equity market during 2023. For reference, the Northbound Stock Connect recorded US\$7.72 billion net outflows into mainland China in 2023, compared with US\$13.12 billion in 2022 and US\$67.10 billion in 2021. Second, net fund inflows rose to HK\$389 billion in 2023 from HK\$88 billion in 2022, remaining at subdued levels (Figure 3). Third, the majority of the net fund inflows went into private banking and private wealth management business (HK\$341 billion in 2023), which was the opposite case before 2021. The increase in AUM was partially offset by the decline in the values of clients’ portfolio assets, and the AUM of PB & private wealth mgmt business only rose by 0.6%y/y to HK\$9.0 trillion. Last but not least, Mainland China-related business outperformed, as the AUM of the asset and wealth mgmt business of Mainland-related LCs/RIs increased 4%y/y to HK\$2.8 trillion, up 50% since 2019.

Figure 2: AUM of asset managed in HK by location of investment
![](images/6279299265cd65dc1545723d2f5e91d4ea9df2868b64367c304f24487f1c5f9b.jpg)
Source: SFC, J.P. Morgan. Note: excl. fund advisory business and REITs

![](images/74169a038e57f59a38b34a2cc4f4725e76f9d35ee9c5275f9f6556f81d2e4fb5.jpg)
Source: SFC, J.P. Morgan
Note: excl. REITs and assets held under trusts attributable to non-LCs/RI

More broadly, global assets under management increased by 12%y/y and Asia AUM trended up by 8%y/y in 2023, according to the Monetary Authority of Singapore (link). Singapore’s total AUM of asset management business increased 10%y/y to SG\$5.4 trillion (or HK\$31.5 trillion) by end-2023 (Figure 4). The net fund inflows narrowed to SG\$193 billion (or HK\$1.1 trillion) in 2023, compared with SG\$435 billion (or HK\$2.5 trillion), reflecting the challenges amidst continued market volatility and investor caution. In addition, the AUM data from HK and SG may not be directly comparable, as the methods of classification may differ between the two financial hubs. Nevertheless, the tale of two cities of the asset management business in Asia has just started, offering more diversified opportunities for regional and global investors.

Figure 4: Asset & wealth management business in SG and HK HK\$ trillion, both scales
![](images/d4d5d5c92dd54cd0003d0e38d67b99f82d6d8dffcf4495c49ba26a5b6300df1d.jpg)
Source: SFC, MAS, J.P. Morgan. Note: Net fund inflows for HK excludes REITs and assets held under trusts attributable to non-LCs/RIs. Data from HK and SG may not be directly comparable.

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
